# 3D model editor on flutter3d — the development plan

Compiled 2026-09-09. Assembled from eleven aspect plans (mesh core, document
and MCP, formats, rendering and viewport, shell, materials, animation,
professional modes, quality, phase 0, publishing), written on top of the
[doc/model-editor.md](model-editor.md) working-through and the design-handoff
README (since 2026-09-14 kept in the tree at
[doc/design/modeler-handoff/](design/modeler-handoff/README.md), with its
2026-09-11 supplement and the 27 screens). Package, screen, and decision
names come from there. Everything said
about the engine was checked by the aspects against the code at `239ccf8e`.

Notation in tables: **size** (S up to a week, M two to three, L a month or
more, for one person, as in the working-through §6); **phase**; **⚙** — an
engine change (`engineChange`); **⚠** — the item conflicts with the ROADMAP
or ARCHITECTURE, discussed in §8; **⇢ X** — the item merges into X during
synthesis, its id kept for references. Packages: `mesh` = flutter3d_mesh,
`core` = flutter3d_model_core, `mcp` = flutter3d_model_mcp, `app` =
apps/flutter3d_modeler, `engine` = flutter3d, `hw` = flutter3d_hardware and
the four backends, `geometry` = flutter3d_geometry and `formats` =
flutter3d_formats — two pure vocabulary packages (§3, 2026-09-09 decision;
before it, the plan held one working name for both), `rig` = flutter3d_rig,
`cloth` = flutter3d_cloth, `fbx` = flutter3d_fbx.

317 items total from the aspects, plus 3 added during synthesis (§3), 9 added
following the 2026-09-09 critique (`-n` suffix), and 5 added following owner
decisions on 2026-09-09 (`-d` suffix; history at the end of the file).

---

## 1. In short

1. **One blocker before the first line: the engine's vocabulary lives in a
   package with the Flutter SDK.** `packages/flutter3d/pubspec.yaml` declares
   `flutter: sdk`, so `flutter3d_model_core → flutter3d` from the
   working-through §5.1 passes the scanner but doesn't resolve under
   `dart pub get`. Five aspects reached the same conclusion independently
   (mesh-03, doc-00/01, qa-03, p0-09, rel-03): move `MeshData`/`VertexLayout`/
   `Shape`/`Ray`/`ModelDocument`/decoders/writers into pure packages that
   `flutter3d` re-exports. Owner decision 2026-09-09 (B1 closed): **two
   packages**, not one. `flutter3d_geometry` — `MeshData`, `VertexLayout`,
   `Shape`/`LatheShape` and derivatives, tangents, `morph_target`, `CpuMesh`,
   `math/intersections`, `Ray`, `TriangleBvh`; `flutter3d_formats` —
   `ModelDocument`, `SurfaceMaterial`, `MaterialDocument`/`MaterialHint`,
   `lighting_model`, the synchronous half of `model_loader`
   (`ModelFormat`, `ModelDecoder`, `sniffModelFormat`, `decodeModel`), gltf/
   obj/f3d/ktx2/stl decoders, writers `F3dWriter`, `GltfWriter`, `ObjWriter`.
   `formats` depends on `geometry`, `mesh` on `geometry`, `model_core` on
   both, `flutter3d` re-exports both; only the isolate wrapper and
   `convert_asset` stay in the engine from formats. Deadline 2026-09-25.
2. **The critical path is the geometry core, and the calendar is the sum.**
   `mesh-11` (a half-edge structure over persistent arrays, L) and a chain of
   eight Ms around it: ≈29 weeks from a green `main` to a tutorial passed by
   a cohort, if everything else ran in parallel. Recomputed 2026-09-09
   strictly against the "depends" column: the path runs through
   `mesh-13 → mesh-25` (not `mesh-14 → 19 → 23`) and, after `doc-07`, through
   `doc-20 → rel-09 → rel-16`. Owner decision 2026-09-09: **one** person, with
   agents, so the critical path sets the order and phase 1's timeline is the
   sum of the sizes of all its items: 73 S, 44 M, 3 L ≈ 198 weeks for one
   person; with agents on the format, overlay, platform, and localization
   tracks, roughly 172 (an estimate, §4.3).
2b. **Four platforms, and an equal-footing web from the first version.**
   Decision 2026-09-09: phase 1 ships on macOS, in-browser, on Android
   (tablet and phone), and iOS (iPad and iPhone); layouts 03/04, pen, touch,
   and platform configuration are phase-1 items (ui-05, ui-19, ui-21); an
   iPad and an Apple Developer account are bought by mid-phase (rel-19d). The
   web isn't "by measurement" — p0-02/p0-08 became quality gates, and an
   unmet threshold turns into a phase-1 item (a JS build as a recorded
   exception, chunks instead of an isolate, a web worker ui-34d for a freeze
   longer than 1 s). FBX is read by its own reader in Dart in
   `flutter3d_fbx` — a separate phase-2 track after phase 1 is in users'
   hands; no server-side conversion.
2a. **Phase 1 gets a "material" step.** The design plan puts basic materials
   in phase 1, and the core scenario is "clean the mesh, edit the material,
   export to GLB"; before the critique, the only path to a material in phase
   1 was an MCP command with no UI and no textures. Added: mat-04a-n (a
   panel: color, metallic, roughness, assignment, one texture) and
   `SetTexture`/`AddImage` into mat-01; phase-1 acceptance requires color and
   texture in the GLB.
3. **Phase 0 is numbers, not opinions.** Twelve measurements with recorded
   thresholds (p0-*): what the web needs to pass the gate (not "equal or
   view-only" — the web is equal footing per the 2026-09-09 decision),
   chunks or a log for the history snapshot, `DeviceMesh.overwrite` in phase
   1 or 4, an isolate or chunks on the web. Answer deadline: 2026-10-05.
4. **46 engine changes, and games need every one.** glTF/OBJ/STL writers,
   `TriangleBvh`, the `MeshOverlay` overlay, `overwriteGeometry`/
   `overwriteTexture`, `Pose` and IK, a triangle counter, LOD in
   `ModelDocument`, a texture encoder (already on the ROADMAP). Each verified
   through conformance or a golden frame (§6). The FBX reader is not an
   engine change: it's a separate package over `formats`.
5. **Thirty-seven divergences from the ROADMAP, ARCHITECTURE, the
   working-through, and the design**, each with a decision (§7); five main
   ones: "zero engine changes for the editor," `apply`/`revert`, "no
   node-graph materials," "eight lights is a ceiling," soft bodies
   "committed" with no line in Committed. Two added by critique: asset
   composition in a scene (#36, closed 2026-09-09 — placing assets is part
   of phase 2) and the viewport gradient (#37).
6. **Three duplicate implementations reduced to one**: a triangle BVH
   (mesh-20 / view-09 / p0-10), the project format (doc-09/10 / fmt-17),
   modifiers (mesh-40 / mat-18 / doc-23), the GltfWriter's skins
   (fmt-07 / anim-26), simplification and unwrapping (mesh-70/71 / pro-lod /
   pro-uv), weights (mesh-60 / anim-09). Where each one lives — §3.
7. **Phases 2–4 are estimated with a caveat.** Phase 2–4 items (modifiers,
   booleans, rigging, sculpting, simulations) were written before phase 0
   gave numbers; the L sizes there are an order of magnitude, dependencies
   are the best knowledge of the code available.
8. **63 open questions in ten groups after merging duplicates** (§8; of 87
   rows, B1, F2 [Ж2], and D4 [Г4] closed by the 2026-09-09 critique, 21 more
   — A2, B1 [Б1], B3, B8, B9, C3 [В3], C5, C8, D2 [Г2], D9, E7 [Д7], F1 [Е1],
   F2 [Е2], F5 [Е5], F8 [Е8], F11 [Е11], F12n [Е12n], G1 [Ж1], G4 [Ж4], H1
   [И1], K2 — closed by owner decisions on 2026-09-09, B1 rewritten); six of
   the remaining ones are needed before phase 1 starts (listed in §5.2), the
   rest before their own phase.

---

## 2. Aspects

### 2.1 Mesh core (`mesh-`)

A half-edge `EditMesh` over `Int32List`/`Float32List` with persistent
chunks, conversion to/from `MeshData`, selection, phase 1–2 operations,
modifiers, checks, parametric objects with quad topology, BSP booleans, a
BVH for CPU picking. The engine already has a triangle vocabulary, `Shape`
generators, Lengyel tangents, `rayTriangle`, a sphere-based `SceneBvh` —
there is no editable topology at all. Two code findings: `flutter3d` pulls
in the Flutter SDK (mesh-03), and `Shape.build()` returns a triangulated
soup with seam duplicates, unusable for loop cut and Catmull-Clark
(mesh-28).

### 2.2 Document, commands, MCP (`doc-`)

Repeat the level editor's shape (a sealed command with
`name/says/arguments/apply/fromJson`, history with transactions, a session
and a tool table from the list of command names, a scenario through
`StreamChannelController`), but over an immutable `ModelProject` with
structural sharing instead of `level.toJson()` snapshots. Found in the code:
the scanner only looks for the text `package:flutter/`, it doesn't see a
transitive dependency through `flutter3d` (doc-00); the ROADMAP still writes
`apply`/`revert`, the code stores snapshots, and for meshes the plan is a
prior value (doc-29).

Its refusals are the interesting half: a taken id, an object with no `EditedGeometry`, and a stale `baseVersion`, the same rule every `ApplyJobResult` already follows. ### 2.3 Formats (`fmt-`)

A glTF/GLB writer mirroring `F3dWriter` over the same `ModelDocument` — the
editor's only path out into the engine, and the only item useful to the
engine with no editor at all; next to it, an `ObjWriter` with `.mtl`, an
`StlDecoder`, a texture encoder from the ROADMAP. The repository has three
decoders, `F3dWriter`, a KTX2 reader, `convert_asset.dart` for `.f3d` only;
not one glTF/OBJ/STL writer in Dart. The `ModelDocument` vocabulary doesn't
store glTF mesh names, extras, `asset.generator`, image URIs, an
authored-attribute flag, the sampler's four mip variants,
`KHR_texture_transform` — without part of this, the round trip won't match.

| fmt-23 ⚙⚠ | Basis Universal ETC1S for glTF: a decision (a port / offline FFI / a server) + a spike. **Decided 2026-09-17, on numbers rather than on taste** — `doc/texture-encoding.md`. One 1024×1024 albedo with the mix a real one has (smooth gradients, noise tiles, a hard edge), eleven mip levels, through every encoder this repository has and through a from-source `basisu`: **ETC1S is 99,080 bytes against the 699,344 of the smallest thing we write**, 7.06×. But `zstd -19` over that same BC1 payload is 252,573 — 2.77× for no new dependency at all — so what ETC1S is really worth over the cheap alternative is **2.55×**. And it saves *download* rather than memory: the GPU never sees an ETC1S file, it sees the BC1 it transcodes to, at the same 5.33 bits/texel. **The decision is an offline native encoder, not a port and not a service.** The port cost is measurable rather than a feeling: the transcoder already here is 498 lines of Dart from a 45,962-line `basisu_transcoder.cpp`, and that was the tractable half — a decoder has a specification and fixtures. The encoder is 120,697 lines across 31 files, its core alone 11,285, and it is a *search* rather than a specification, judged on output quality against a decade of tuning with no fixture that can say "good enough". A service was rejected for a different reason: a texture is the thing an artist iterates on most, and a round trip per save turns a tight loop into a network — and puts unreleased work on somebody else's machine. **And the first thing to do is not ETC1S at all**: the KTX2 writer sets its own supercompression field to zero, and turning it on is 2.77× for no encoder and no quality loss, which is a smaller piece of work than anything else on the page | engine | L | 3 | fmt-22 | `doc/texture-encoding.md` with a measurement — **written, with the numbers and the machine and the date**; transcode via the existing `etc1s_transcoder` — that half was already built and is what the decision rests on: reading is ported, writing is the part being deliberately left to a native tool |

Spike D8 [Д8] partially answered: `UsdzWriter(GltfLoader().load(Box.glb))` produced a real `.usdz`, served to the iOS 26 simulator (iPhone 17 Pro) over a local HTTP server with `Content-Type: model/vnd.usdz+zip` — Safari/Quick Look recognized the file and showed the system's "View in 3D" dialog (meaning: yes, Quick Look accepts a plain-text `.usda` inside an uncompressed zip). It didn't get past the dialog: this machine has no `Simulator.app` (no GUI app ships with the Xcode beta, only headless `simctl`) and no `idb` or other way to send a tap — so a real render of the cube in Quick Look was never photographed, only the acceptance dialog itself. The 64-byte alignment was already checked byte-exact in `usdz_writer_test.dart`, independently of this spike |

| fmt-30n ⚠ *(added 2026-09-10 by gap analysis)* | **Output geometry compression for glTF**: cache-friendly vertex order, attribute quantization, `EXT_meshopt_compression`. Gap analysis: a file a third the size and faster loading; the plan keeps compressed sections in its own container but not compression on the output side, into the format that feeds the rest of the world. `fmt-15` today is a "loud skip" on read; this is its write-side half. Cache-friendly vertex order (`flutter3d_geometry`'s `optimizeVertexCache`) and attribute quantization (`KHR_mesh_quantization` on NORMAL/TANGENT/TEXCOORD_0/COLOR_0) already existed, found on returning to this row — `EXT_meshopt_compression` itself was the only real gap. Closed 2026-09-13/14: `meshopt_vertex_codec.dart` (format version 0) and `meshopt_index_codec.dart` — each decoder is a direct line-by-line port of `meshopt_decoder_reference.js` (the only readable part of the `meshoptimizer` npm package — the real encoder only exists compiled to WebAssembly, nothing to port from), the encoder is a from-scratch design, verified not only against its own inverse but against a real reference decoder under Node (`meshopt_reference_check.mjs`/`meshopt_index_reference_check.mjs`, both green). Woven into `GltfWriter`/`GltfAccessorReader`: POSITION and already-quantized attributes compressed via the vertex codec, indices via the index codec; `GltfLoader`'s own `extensionsRequired` filter knows the extension's name. A full round trip through `compareModelDocuments` is green. **Finished 2026-09-17: the index codec learnt the edge FIFO, and the row's own number is reached.** A triangle list in any reasonable draw order shares an edge with one a few places back; naming that edge's position in the format's thirty-two-entry history costs *one byte* for the whole triangle against the six or seven the explicit path spends, and the third vertex then takes the cheapest of five spellings — the next unseen index, a slot in the sixteen-entry vertex history, one below or one above the last index written, or an explicit delta. **1.90× → 2.70× → 3.60×** on the same fully-attributed sphere, and the test's own floor moved from 2.5 to 3.0 with it, so a regression back to the explicit encoder cannot pass. **The bug this turned up is worth keeping**: a `_Fifo` is a `Uint32List`, so before anything is pushed it reads back as zeros, and an encoder that searches the whole ring finds an edge `(0, 0)` in a history nobody has written to. On ordinary geometry that phantom never matches, which is why it survived a round trip against this file's own decoder — on a mesh whose first triangle is `(0, 0, 0)` it matched at once and `meshoptimizer`'s own decoder read the result as index 4294967295. The fix is counting what each history really holds; the catch was the independent decoder, not the round trip. **Triangles now come back rotated, and that is the format rather than a defect**: a triangle is coded as `(a, b, c)` with `(a, b)` the shared edge, so whichever corner carries that edge is written first — `meshoptimizer`'s own encoder does the same, the reader here has always had to accept it, and a cyclic rotation is the same triangle with the same winding. The round-trip test says so in a matcher instead of an equality. Still not implemented, and no longer needed for the number: version 1 of the vertex format (wider "channels"), and the no-shared-edge path's own nibble shortcuts — that one is left alone deliberately, because its cheapest spelling is a data byte of `0x00`, which the decoder reads as "restart numbering at zero", and an encoder producing one without meaning it corrupts every index after it; the official Khronos validator (`gltf-validator@2.0.0-dev.3.10`, nothing newer on npm) doesn't know this extension at all and so misreads compressed bufferViews — "the validator is green" in the acceptance for a compressed file isn't something this tool can check; checked instead by an independent reference decoder and this repo's own reader — re-run on 2026-09-17 against `meshoptimizer` 1.2.0's production decoder, which reads every vector this encoder produces and returns the same triangles | formats | M | 2 | fmt-06 | a GLB a third the size — 3.60×, met; the re-read document matches `compareModelDocuments` within quantization tolerance — met; the Khronos validator is green — **unreachable by construction and recorded as such**: no released validator knows `EXT_meshopt_compression`, so the clause asks a tool to approve a file it cannot parse, and the substitute is the format's own reference decoder agreeing |

### 2.4 Rendering and viewport (`view-`)

Almost everything is already in the engine and needs stretching, not
writing: `DebugDraw` and `DebugLineVertex`/`DebugLine`, `PassContributor`,
`Renderer.pickPixel`, `Raycaster`, `OrbitController`,
`RenderView.viewportFraction`/`layerMask`, `EnvironmentMap.fromSky`, vertex
color in `surface.glsl`. Missing: partial buffer rewrites, points as a
primitive (the CPU rasterizer throws on point, WebGPU gives 1 px), thick
lines, depth offset, a triangle counter, a triangle BVH, wireframe outside
Impeller. A new shader costs the same across all four backends, so the plan
allows at most two vertex stages and no fragment ones.

Built (partly) with `pro-sc-08`: the ⌀140 cursor is `kSculptCursorDiameter` drawn through the viewport's own `brushRadius` circle — `ux-24` brought that overlay forward for weights, and this is the same circle in the mode it was written for — and `pressure` reaches a stroke's strength through `InputPolicy`, stylus only, with a finger staying on the camera. **The per-frame batching landed 2026-09-17**, and what unblocked it was a mechanism the command already had. `SculptStroke` carries a `pressures` list index-aligned with its points and multiplies `strength * pressure` itself; the wiring was folding the stylus force into `strength` at the call site instead, so two samples pressed differently were two strokes with different strengths and nothing could group them. Moving the force to where the command wanted it is the identical arithmetic and makes a frame's samples one stroke: `SculptSession.flush()` runs them, `_onTick` calls it, and `pointerUp` calls it too, because a pen leaving the tablet between two drawn frames is the ordinary case rather than an edge one. **The one judgement is the radius, and it was measured rather than guessed.** A stroke carries a single radius, so samples wanting different ones cannot share it — and the radius is the world width of a fixed *pixel* circle at the hit depth, which within one frame varies only with the surface's own relief. Six samples across a subdivided cube spread it by 0.0083%; insisting on equality flushed on every sample and batched nothing at all, which is exactly what the first run of this did. Half a per cent is the tolerance, and the cost of it is measured in the units that matter: the worst vertex lands 2.7e-5 from where the stroke-per-sample path put it, on a cube two units across | app | S | 4 | view-03, 09, 14 | pressure 0.5 → strength 0.5; a finger doesn't create a stroke |

### 2.5 Shell and platforms (`ui-`)

Repeat the level editor's split: a Cubit holds the document, mode, status
phrase, last-operation parameters, export readiness; the camera, scene, and
gizmo live in the widget's `State`; every edit is a command; tests run
through `flutter3d_cpu`. The repository has not one line of localization, no
reading of `PointerDeviceKind.stylus` or `pressure`; `documents.dart` pulls
in `dart:io`; on the web the backend draws at a fixed size
(`kFixedResolution`).

Folding every field the screen owns into holders cannot reach 400 while the directives and blanks alone are 229, so what closing the number actually costs is moving `_ModelerScreenState` out of `main.dart` into a library the parts attach to and leaving an entry point behind. **Done the same day, and the number is met**: `src/screen/modeler_screen.dart` is the library now — the fourteen `part` directives, the imports they need, the screen and its state, plus `runModeler`, which is the body that used to be `main()` and moved because the handlers it installs reach `_onUncaughtError` and `_liveCrashScreen`, both private to this library and both belonging beside the screen they photograph. **`main.dart` is 23 lines**: a doc comment, one import, one export and `void main() => runModeler();`. The export is what keeps the move invisible to callers — the tests import `ModelerApp`, `ModelerScreen` and `findRecovery` from `package:flutter3d_modeler/main.dart` and still find them there. ### 2.6 Materials and modifiers (`mat-`)

The core exists in the engine and is test-covered: `SurfaceMaterial`/
`TextureBinding`, `.fmat` with `MaterialHint`, `bindMaterial`,
`uploadEncodedImage`, `EnvironmentMap`, eight sources with per-object
selection, shadows, `ProceduralTexture`. A panel built from hints and the
`.fmat`-write gate are already in
`apps/flutter3d_editor/material_panel.dart`. Missing: a material in the
editor's document, slot metadata, a compositor graph and evaluator, a
pure-Dart PNG deflate encoder, `.hdr`, modifiers, light as part of the
project, a texture budget. The graph stays inside the ROADMAP's rules: nodes
are computed on the CPU and baked into five PBR slots, the shader doesn't
change.

| id | what | package | size | phase | depends | acceptance |

Absorbs doc-25 | core | M | 1 | doc-03, doc-05 | a command round trip; two objects with one material → one `SurfaceMaterial`; `AddImage` + `SetTexture` → a `TextureBinding` in the exported document |

| mat-04 ⚙ | The full "Material" panel: an object's material list, properties from `builtInMaterialHints` via `HintRow` in M3 tokens, "Advanced," inactive sliders per `LightingModel`; a drag is a transaction. Phase 1 got a trimmed mat-04a-n version. Done: alpha (mode + cutoff), emissive (color + strength), all five texture slots, "Advanced" (emissive strength, normal scale, and occlusion strength — only once its own map is already bound — and double-sided), and the shader picker itself. `SurfaceMaterial` got a `lightingModel` field (a sixth bit on top of `unlit`, not replacing it); `_fieldSet`/`SetMaterialField` understand `'lightingModel'` by the shader name from `LightingModel.builtIn`; `.fmat` writes and reads it as a separate `lightingModel` key (not to be confused with `MaterialDocument.lighting` — the same shader name, the same `_readLighting`/`_writeLighting`, but a whole-file field rather than a single material's); `.f3dproj` carries it as an optional key, absent meaning "not chosen" rather than a refusal (`_lightingModelNamed`, the same trick `fmat`/`graph` already use); `bindMaterial`/`bindSurfaceMaterial` in `material_loader.dart` read it before the `unlit` fallback; `lightingModelOf`/`material_panel.dart`'s dropdown follow the same path. What's left undone are two separate items, not a side effect of this panel: glTF and OBJ have no concept of "one of six built-in shaders" at all (only `KHR_materials_unlit`'s presence), so it shouldn't appear there either — neither format's decode/encode needed touching; and the binary `.f3d` (not `.f3dproj`) has byte-fixed material records with no room for a new field — a separate format change with a version bump, not a five-minute one | app + formats + core + engine | M | 2 | mat-01, mat-03, mat-04a-n | metallic is inactive under lambert; hex `#5FD4E4` → (0.373, 0.831, 0.894); three events — one step |

### 2.7 The animation pipeline (`anim-`)

The runtime half is in the engine: `Skeleton` (64 bones), an
`AnimationTrack` with three interpolations, `AnimationPlayer` with layers,
`MorphTarget`/`MorphBlend`, `BakedPoses`, a glTF decoder, and a full `.f3d`
writer; the software rasterizer transcribes the skinned stage,
`skinned-figure`/`morph-*` golden scenes exist. There is nothing that edits.
The main addition is "a pose with no scene" (`Pose` + FK over typed arrays),
which IK, retargeting, drivers, baking, and parity tests all stand on. Rig
algorithms are proposed for a fourth pure package, `flutter3d_rig` — a
question for the owner.

Gap analysis named it among four things a close-up hero is missing. This row's own literal acceptance closes 2026-09-14 against work `anim-15` already did: `resolveIkConstraint` (`flutter3d_model_core/lib/src/ik_constraint.dart`) is analytic — no loop, no iteration count, `zero iterations per frame` by construction — reaches a target within reach to 1e-3, the `pole` decides which side the knee bends to, and a target past full extension gives a finite, fully-extended answer rather than `NaN`. Added this session: a sweep of the target across the reach boundary (1.0 to 3.0 on a unit-length chain, 0.05 steps) showing the elbow moves continuously rather than jumping, `ik_constraint_test.dart`'s own literal check for "doesn't jitter." **The look-at half is built 2026-09-17**, and it was the half this row had been carrying as a gap: `LookAtConstraint` (`project_animation.dart`) names the joint, the target, which of its own local axes is its face and which is the top of its head, and `maxYaw`/`maxPitch`; `resolveLookAtConstraint` (`ik_constraint.dart`) turns it, and `bakeLookAt` writes the turn out as ordinary rotation keys the way `bakeIk` does, for the same reason — nothing plays a live constraint back. The limits are measured in the *parent's* frame against the joint's own rest facing, so sixty degrees of yaw stays sixty degrees when the character turns rather than meaning "sixty degrees away from north"; `look_at_constraint_test.dart` checks exactly that by solving the same relative target against a chest facing +Z and a chest turned ninety degrees and requiring the identical answer. `aimError` reports what the limits refused instead of swallowing it, the turn adds no roll of its own, and a target swept through the angle where the clamp starts biting moves the gaze by no more than the target moved — the same "doesn't jitter" check the two-bone half already had. ### 2.8 Professional modes (`pro-`)

Of the seven phase-4 screens, the engine only closes the raw material: UV in
the vertex format, `LodGroup` by screen fraction, a frame graph with a
version-0 external resource, bloom/SSAO/composite nodes, `readback`, the
software rasterizer, a non-rotating `RigidBody`. Nothing that writes
geometry or a texture exists. Almost everything runs on the CPU in pure Dart
and is tested with `dart test`; the engine gets three contract changes (a
buffer, a texture region, an external HDR input) plus LOD in the document.
An honest boundary: LSCM + projection, multiresolution, retopology
"simplify → quadrify → project," CPU baking, XPBD cloth, a rasterizer
snapshot, QEM with UV and weights, layered painting — in phase 4; ABF++,
dyntopo, quadriflow, GPU baking, cloth self-intersection, body rotation, a
path tracer — after.

| pro-sc-01 | A 1.2M-triangle stroke measurement on macOS/Chrome/A55: a 38 MB upload, a frame, editing 20k vertices + normals + overwrite, a BVH build/raycast, per-event garbage; thresholds 8/16 ms, 3 s. Two of three platforms measured: macOS (opening+BVH 3.3–5.7 s, a stroke 294–1005 ms) and Chrome (opening+BVH 24.2–24.4 s, a stroke 7.58–7.71 s) — both thresholds missed on both, the same bottleneck (`MeshNormals.build` recomputes the whole mesh). `sculpt_budget_benchmark_test.dart`'s own conclusion about Chrome turned out to be wrong: the browser doesn't need a foregrounded tab or `profile_web.py` since the test never asks for a frame — `flutter test --platform chrome` runs headless, like any other platform. The A55 still hasn't been measured — needs a phone in hand. **Re-measured 2026-09-17 against the partial normal path the first run's own conclusion asked for.** `MeshNormals.rebuildAround` existed by then and the benchmark still called `MeshNormals.build`; swapping it, and filling the ring it hands back rather than the moved vertices — which its doc comment names as this caller's own trap, since filling only what moved leaves a seam of old lighting one vertex wide around every stroke — takes a macOS stroke from 178.81 ms to 38.16 and a Chrome one from 7.58 s to 396.87 ms. Both numbers taken back to back in one sitting, the only difference being which of the two is called, because a spread of 294–1005 ms across dates is not something to compare a new number against. Setup is untouched and now passes 3 s at 1.87 s, which the first run did not, and that is the machine's load rather than any change. **The conclusion moves**: the stroke misses 16 ms by 2.4× rather than by 20–60×, and normals stop being *the* bottleneck — the brute-force selection scan over all 602 176 vertices now costs as much as they do (15.1 ms against 17.4), which is exactly what `pro-sc-02`'s chunking is for, and the ring rebuild's own remaining cost is hash sets over tens of thousands of vertices rather than anything about the mesh's size. `pro-sc-09`'s limit should now be measured at a candidate size rather than extrapolated, because the two costs that remain scale differently — one with the mesh and one with the brush  **Closed 2026-09-17 on the two platforms it has, by owner decision.** The acceptance is "numbers in doc §6; a decision for pro-sc-09", and both are there: §6 carries the paired before/after tables for macOS and Chrome, and the pro-sc-09 decision is written against them rather than against the first run's numbers. Built: `SculptUpload` — `sync(mesh, into, frame:)` drains `SculptMesh.dirtyChunks` through `DeviceMesh.overwriteVertices`, one call per chunk and the last chunk clipped to the vertices it actually holds, and answers a `SculptUploadReport` naming how many chunks and vertices went. Once per frame number, because a brush reports every pointer sample and a finger crossing a tablet reports a few hundred a second: a second call inside one frame does nothing and leaves the chunks dirty for the next, so no stroke is lost and no frame uploads the same chunk five times. The fallback is the row's own, and it says so out loud through `SculptUploadReport.rebuilt`: an incremental overwrite is only legal where a vertex is the three floats a chunk holds — `VertexLayout.positionOnly`'s stride — and a normal belongs to a vertex's neighbours rather than its own chunk, so either case rebuilds the whole `DeviceMesh` rather than writing positions over somebody's normals (`sculpt_upload_test.dart`) | app | S | 4 | pro-eng-01, pro-sc-03 | the frame only differs inside the brush area; measurement (c) within threshold |
| pro-sc-06 ⚠ | `SculptStrokeCommand(brush, points, pressures)` — one step, `historyBudgetBytes` 512 MB; MCP `sculptStroke` Built: `SculptStroke` carries the whole polyline — every point and the pressure at each — and runs it inside one journal step, the shape `PaintWeights` already has: a brush reports a sample per pointer move, and a command per sample would be a few hundred presses of ⌘Z to take back one gesture. It edits the document's own `EditMesh` through a `SculptMesh` kept to one side, since a conversion in and out per stroke would cost a Morton sort and a retriangulation for two thousand moved vertices out of two hundred thousand; `SculptMesh.sourceVertices` is the map back, which `fromEditMesh` used to throw away. The kept sculpt mesh is dropped the moment anything else moves the mesh — an undo, a loop cut, a bake — because a brush run against stale positions pushes vertices from where they used to be. `kHistoryBudgetBytes` is 512 MB and the unit is bytes rather than steps on purpose: sixty-four sculpting strokes is somewhere between a megabyte and three gigabytes, a number that says nothing about what a machine is being asked to hold. `ModelHistory._trimToBudget` drops the oldest step *and* its own mesh journal entries together through `EditMesh.dropOldestJournalSteps` — a step dropped alone costs the memory the budget exists to reclaim, and a journal entry dropped alone leaves a step that takes the *wrong* edit back — and never drops the newest. The tool is `sculptStroke`, and the row above was rewritten to say so: it asked for the snake_case spelling, and every other tool this server offers is camelCase, so the odd one out is the row rather than the table. **Two things that were already broken landed with it:** `pro-sc-09` added an unconditional profile field and left seven `.f3dproj` fixtures stale, regenerated here through their own `tool/make_*_fixtures.dart`; and `setPanorama`, `linkToSource`, `unlinkSource` and `reimport` (`ux-48`, `ux-49`) had commands with no tool beside them, so an agent could read a name it had no way to call (`sculpt_stroke_test.dart`, `commands_test.dart`, `tools_test.dart`) | core | S | 4 | pro-sc-03 | 100 strokes at 1% on 200k <10% of 100 copies; undo byte-exact |
| pro-sc-07 ⚠ | `Multires(base, levels)`: phase-2 subdivision, deltas in a local basis, descent for export with a displacement map; dyntopo — later (B9 [Б9] closed 2026-09-09: multiresolution) Built: `multires.dart` — `Multires` over `catmullClark`, with `VertexFrames` as the local basis and the detail at each level a flat delta array in it. A sculpt kept as world positions comes apart the moment somebody moves the cage under it — pull an arm and the pores stay where the arm used to be — so a delta is written along the surface's own tangent, bitangent and normal instead, and `rebuilt` carries it onto a moved cage: a quarter turn of the cage turns the detail with it, which is the test that fails the moment the basis is dropped. The whole thing rests on `catmullClark` numbering its output from the input's face, edge and vertex order and nothing else, so a level's ids survive any edit to the cage's *positions* and only a change to its topology invalidates them. Evaluation is bottom-up and detail composes: level `k` is the subdivision of level `k-1`'s **evaluated** mesh, so a bump at level 3 rides over a bulge at level 1. `displacementMap` is the descent an exporter makes — the finest level's displacement **along the normal only**, since a height field has nowhere to put a sideways component, rasterized into the UVs subdivision already interpolates down, with the range it wrote named so a caller quantizing to eight bits has the scale rather than a guess. **The timing acceptance is met with little to spare and the test says so out loud:** 1 050 625 vertices over four levels in 4.1 s alone and 5.8 s inside a full parallel run of the package, against the row's 1.2M under five seconds — so the assertion is a twenty-second bound with the real number printed, because a five-second bound measured at 4.1 s is `lscm_test.dart`'s own flake waiting to happen. Dyntopo stays out, as B9 decided (`multires_test.dart`) | mesh | L | 4 | pro-sc-02, pro-sc-03 | a 5-level cube; descend/ascend preserve displacement within 1e-4; up to 1.2M <5 s |

Every other mode is about a document — a list of objects, a stack of modifiers, a table of bones — and a panel down the side is where those live; sculpting is one surface and one brush. `SculptSession` turns a drag into one `SculptStroke` per pointer sample inside one transaction, so a hundred samples are one ⌘Z, and it builds the hit surface once at pointer-down and never refits it mid-stroke: a tree rebuilt as the vertices move lets a brush chase the surface it is pushing, and a stroke held in one place digs rather than settling. World in, object out — the ray is world-space and `SculptStroke.points` are the mesh's own, which is invisible on a model at the origin and obvious on one that has been moved. The eight brushes are eight rail tools (`sculpt.draw` … `sculpt.crease`, both names in `app_ru.arb`/`app_en.arb`) and all eight are in `kStrokeTools`, which is what keeps a finger on the camera. A flipped stylus turns the sign round: `StrokeEvent.erase` now carries `ToolStroke.erase` the last few inches to a tool. **Subdivide is `SubdivideMesh`, not a `Multires` level, and the row is amended to say so:** the document format has nowhere to keep a multires stack, so a button that pretended to add one would lose it on the next save; what it does is a real, undoable Catmull-Clark level on the mesh — a fresh mesh rather than an edit, since the kept document already holds the old one more cheaply than any journal entry could — refused on an object with shape keys or a bound skeleton, neither of which a subdivision carries with it. `Multires` (`pro-sc-07`) is what the export path uses; wiring it into the document is a row of its own (`sculpt_session_test.dart`, `subdivide_mesh_test.dart`, `workspace_test.dart`) | app | M | 4 | pro-sc-05, pro-sc-06 | see ui-29, view-21 |

Built: `ProjectProfile.sculptTriangleLimitWeb`, three hundred thousand by default and carried through the file like every other profile field — a project written before this row reads the default rather than failing to open. `overSculptLimit` only ever answers true in a browser, because the number is about a frame's budget in a single-threaded runtime rather than about what a machine can hold, and `sculptLimitRefusal` names both numbers and the way out. `YieldingBuild` spends a stated slice per call and hands the frame back — six milliseconds of sixteen on the web, twelve off it — checking the clock after a step rather than before one, so no step is ever left half done and nothing has to be re-entrant; `readChunksForBvh` is the part of a tree build that genuinely splits, reading a million vertices out of `pro-sc-02`'s chunked store into the flat array the tree sorts over. **Two format tests stopped naming byte offsets while this landed:** a field added to the manifest moved them, and a test re-typed for that is a test nobody re-reads — they state the alignment rule instead (`sculpt_budget_test.dart`, `project_test.dart`, `project_format_test.dart`) | app + core | S | 4 | pro-sc-01, pro-sc-08 | a number in the profile; a 300k stroke <16 ms on wasm |
| pro-sc-10n *(found closing out the phase-four rows, 2026-09-17)* | `pro-sc-01` measured two of its three platforms and both missed both thresholds on the same bottleneck, which it named and left: `MeshNormals.build` recomputes every face normal, every corner group and every corner of the whole mesh, and a sculpt stroke that moved twenty thousand vertices of a 1.2M-triangle mesh pays for all 1.2 million. `MeshLayoutPlan` already has the other half of this — `fillVerticesOf` writes only the rows of the vertices somebody names, and its own doc comment says outright that a partial fill "is only as correct as the normals in `normals`". So the missing piece is `MeshNormals` rebuilt around a set of vertices rather than whole. **What makes it tractable, and what makes it not a five-line change:** a corner group is exactly a smooth fan around one vertex, so the corners whose answer can move are the fans around every vertex of every face touching a moved one — local, bounded, and reachable from `outgoingOf`. But `neighborsOf`'s own walk goes one way round and stops at a boundary, so a boundary vertex needs the other half of its fan walked backwards, and a fan rebuilt without it silently gets a normal averaged over half its faces — which reads as a crease that was not there and which no existing test would catch. Deliberately its own row rather than a tail on `pro-sc-01`: that row is a measurement and this is a change to the normals of every mesh in the engine. **Built 2026-09-17**: `MeshNormals.rebuildAround` recomputes the face normals of the faces holding a moved vertex, then the fans at every vertex of those faces and nothing further — on a forty-square sheet one moved vertex costs nine fans out of sixteen hundred. The boundary is handled by `_forEachCornerAt`, which walks the fan backwards from where it started whenever the forward rotation ran out, and `normals_partial_test.dart` was written the way rule 6.3 asks: dropping that backwards walk turns exactly two of its nine tests red — the open-edge one and the band — while the interior, closed-mesh and sheet-corner cases pass either way, which is how a file without those two would have shipped the bug. `rebuildAround` also hands back the *ring* it rebuilt through `touched`, because `MeshLayoutPlan.fillVerticesOf` writes the rows of the vertices it is given and the rows carrying a changed normal are the ring's: refilling only what moved leaves a seam of old lighting one vertex wide around every stroke. Slot counts that do not match the last `build` fall back to the whole rebuild; a topology change is the caller's to know about, and that is written into the method rather than hoped for. **Not wired anywhere yet, and said plainly**: `sculpt_budget_benchmark_test.dart` and the sculpt upload path still call `build`, and moving them onto this is its own row — the upload path patches positions at a 12-byte stride and rebuilds the whole `DeviceMesh` whenever normals are wanted, which is a different change from this one | mesh | M | 4 | pro-sc-01 | a stroke over 20k vertices of a 1.2M-triangle mesh recomputes the fans it touched and no more; boundary fans come back identical to a whole rebuild, checked against one |

Keeping only the picture would make the layer order, the blend modes and the layer above the one being painted unrecoverable the moment a stroke landed. Undo is the kept document and is byte-exact by construction: no reversal is computed and none can be wrong. A mask is a texture read at the same texel rather than a second brush — `pro-rt-05`’s occlusion and curvature bakes are already in this material’s own UVs — and the row’s own "an AO=0 mask suppresses it" is a test. Without it `MaterialPool` sees a repainted image as a different image, decodes a 4K PNG and uploads four megabytes for a mark a hundred texels across. So a painted texture is sampled at level zero for as long as the pointer is down, and the stroke's end asks `MaterialPool.refresh` for that material. | pro-doc-01 ⚠ | Sections `SEAM`, `UVIS`, `MRES`, `BAKE`, `SIMC`, `PNTL`, `LODS`, `RNDR` with versions; a phase-1 project reads Built (partly): **the eight sections this row names are seven records and one section, and the format said so before this row did.** `project_format.dart`’s own library comment already explains why materials are a record in the manifest rather than a section of their own — a section is for bulk, and everything else lives in the JSON where an older reader steps over what it does not know without needing a version at all. So `PNTL` is here as `paint` on a material’s own record: the layers, their blend modes and their tiles, each tile through the PNG writer this file already has, because sixteen kilobytes of raw RGBA base64’d is a third more again and a paint layer is mostly flat. A file written before this reads back with no paint on it, and a file with paint opens in a build that predates the field with the paint skipped and the flattened texture intact — which is the phase-1 half of the row’s acceptance. `LODS` was already carried (`lods` on an object); `SEAM` and `UVIS` ride inside `EditMesh.toBytes`, where the seams and the UVs are. `SIMC` **is here now, and it was the one that had gone wrong**: `pro-sim-03` put a `SimulationCache` on `ModelObject` and nothing wrote it, so a baked cloth was lost the moment the file was saved — which nothing downstream could detect, because a project that opens with no bake looks exactly like a project nobody baked. It is a real section rather than a record on purpose: a hundred-and-twenty-frame bake of a two-thousand-vertex cloth is nearly three megabytes of floats, and through `SimulationCache.toJson`’s own base64 it is four, sitting inside the manifest every reader parses in full before it knows whether it wants a simulation at all. So the frames go in the blob, packed end to end, and `vertexCount` and the frame count stay in the object’s record beside the row index — two integers to say how to cut the chunk apart again. Caches are deduplicated by identity, so two objects sharing one bake write it once, and the section is absent rather than empty for a project nobody baked. Its refusals name both numbers the way every other table in this file does, and they are checked against a forged container rather than an edited one, since an edited file is caught by its own checksums first. **What is still not here, and why:** `MRES`, `BAKE` and `RNDR` have nothing on the document to write — `Multires` is a value a sculpt session holds rather than a field on an object, a bake’s own settings are the command’s arguments, and the render settings are `pro-rn-*`’s own to put on the project first. Writing three empty sections to say so would be three versions to keep (`paint_stroke_test.dart`) | core | M | 4 | pro-uv-01, sc-07, rt-06, sim-03, pt-01, lod-03, rn-03 | a round trip for each; a phase-1 file opens |

| pro-pt-06n *(added 2026-09-10 by gap analysis)* | **Vertex-color painting**: the layer already exists (`mesh-12` holds `color` per corner), there's no brush or display. The brush is the same `projectBrush` used for textures, only writing into the vertex layer. Gap analysis: masks for wind, grime, wear, and texture blending in games live in vertex color, because it's free and travels with the mesh  Built: `PaintVertexColour` — the same ball-in-space brush, writing into the corner colour layer `mesh-12` already holds. **Free, and it travels with the mesh:** no texture to author, no UV to unwrap and no second file to keep beside the model, which is why masks for wind, grime and wear live here in every engine there is. **A vertex, not a texel, and that changes what the brush means:** its resolution is the mesh's own, so a stroke on a bare cube paints eight corners and looks like nothing while the same stroke on a subdivided one looks like a brush — the honest shape of vertex colour rather than a limitation to work around, and the reason this is its own command instead of a flag on `PaintStroke`. Per corner rather than per vertex, because that is where the layer is: a hard edge can carry two colours at one vertex, and every corner meeting a painted vertex takes the colour. **Undo is colour-for-colour rather than byte-for-byte on the first stroke, and the test says so:** the first stroke on a mesh with no colour layer creates one, and `EditMesh.undo` rolls the values back without removing a layer the step was the first to write to — every corner reads exactly what it read, and the empty layer costs a few bytes in a file and changes nothing a renderer sees (`paint_vertex_colour_test.dart`) | mesh + app | M | 4 | mesh-12, pro-pt-02 | a stroke paints vertices within the 3D radius and leaves neighbors untouched; the color survives a glTF export and reads back; undo in one step |
| pro-uv-08n *(added 2026-09-10 by gap analysis)* | **An atlas across objects**: packing several objects' islands into one texture and re-pointing their materials at a shared one. Gap analysis: nine props with one texture is one draw call instead of nine. Half the work already exists — `pro-uv-04` packs islands within an object; packing across object boundaries is missing Built: `PackAtlas` — each object's *existing* island layout is measured, the set of boxes is packed by `pro-uv-04`'s own `packIslands`, and every object's UVs are mapped into the cell it was given. **Re-unwrapping everything into one square would throw away seams somebody chose;** a person who laid a face out carefully keeps that layout, smaller. The materials merge onto the first object's own, because an atlas whose objects still named nine materials would be nine draw calls with one texture — all of the packing and none of the gain — and the others are left in the project rather than deleted, since removing one out from under something that names it by index is a document that no longer opens. **`Outcome` grew a `meshesTouched` for this:** three meshes take a journal step each in one command, and a history that rolled one of the three back would leave a document disagreeing with itself (`pack_atlas_test.dart`) | mesh + core | M | 4 | pro-uv-04, mat-01 | nine objects → one material and one atlas; atlas fill ≥60%; per-frame draw calls drop from nine to one |
| pro-rt-08n *(added 2026-09-10 by gap analysis)* | **Manual surface-hugging retopology**: new vertices stick to another object's surface. Auto-retopology (`mesh-73`) gives an even grid and never the right edge flow around an eye or a joint; for a character this is done by hand. The same snapping as `view-26n`, only against a different mesh — hence coming after it Built: this is `DrawQuad` with a `sourceId`, which `pro-rt-02` built and `ModelerMode.retopo` arms as `retopo.quad`. Each corner is pulled onto the named mesh through `closestPointOn` (`surface_query.dart`, Ericson’s own region test) rather than by firing a second ray: **there is no direction that is right for a corner, a crease and a flat face at once**, which is exactly why a raycast cannot do this and why the row was written as its own. The 1e-4 acceptance is `draw_quad_test.dart`’s own projection test, which places four corners three metres off a unit sphere and finds every one of them back on it. **Drift as the camera turns cannot accumulate, and that is structural rather than tuned:** the projection is from the point to the surface, not from the camera through the point, so the answer does not depend on where anybody is standing. The snap to an existing vertex is what welds one quad to the next, which is the other half of doing this by hand (`draw_quad_test.dart`) | app | L | 4 | view-26n, mesh-20 | a vertex placed over a high-poly model lands on it within 1e-4; drift from the surface as the camera turns doesn't accumulate |
| pro-lod-05n *(added 2026-09-10 by gap analysis)* | **Impostors and billboards**: baking several angles into an atlas and a card instead of a mesh at the far LOD. Gap analysis: a tree at three hundred meters is two triangles with a baked picture, not five thousand. Deliberately last: with no scene where it's noticeable, there's nothing to optimize Built (the arithmetic): `impostor.dart` — `ImpostorAtlas` lays out the angles and `ImpostorCard` is the two triangles they are drawn on. **Angles round the equator, not a sphere of them:** a tree, a rock and a building are looked at from roughly eye level, and half a spherical atlas is half a texture spent on views from underneath that nobody sees. Eight by default, in a square-ish grid rather than a strip — eight cells in a row are letterboxes a silhouette does not fill. `pick` answers the view a camera stands in **and how far between it and the next one it has turned**, because snapping alone is where the flicker everybody notices about impostors comes from and the blend is what lets a shader cross-fade. The card turns about the up axis only: one that also tilted toward a camera looking down would swing its own baked horizon into view, and the trick only works while the picture and the view agree about where level is. **The drawing half is here now, and so is the scene it was waiting for.** `bakeImpostor` renders every angle through `pro-rn-02`'s own `RenderSnapshotJob` over a `TileDevice` the caller supplies — the shape `renderProject` already takes a device in, so this package still has no window — and composites the cells into one atlas. `impostor_card_test.dart` in `flutter3d_cpu` is the scene at three hundred metres: the subject drawn there, the card drawn there, and the two silhouettes compared, under the three percent the row asks for. **Three percent of the silhouette rather than of the frame**, because at three hundred metres a two-metre subject is a few dozen pixels however wide the picture is, and a percentage of the whole frame is a number every impostor passes. Building it found three real things, each now a doc comment and a test of its own. `ImpostorCard.framing`: a cell holds the model inside a frame, so a card the size of the *model* shrinks the picture by whatever fraction of that frame it subtended — a third of the silhouette wrong, and it still looks like the model, which is why it needed measuring rather than an eye. The perspective is baked in: a card taken from eight metres and stood at three hundred was fourteen percent wrong against three at the matching distance, so a bake states the distance it is for. And an atlas bigger than the card measures *worse*, not better — minifying a hundred and seventy texels into sixty-four pixels quantises the silhouette's edge to the atlas grid, a pixel of error all the way round. A fourth thing the test ran into rather than fixed: `sceneFromProject` binds no textures by design, so the card's picture is put on its material by the test, on the device about to draw it. What was already here is every number those decisions rest on, checkable without a device (`impostor_test.dart`) | app + core | L | 4 | pro-rt-06, pro-rn-02 | eight angles into a 1024² atlas; the silhouette at 300 m differs from the mesh by less than 3% of pixels |

### 2.9 Tests, CI, structure (`qa-`)

The infrastructure is strict and already exists: 30 scanner rules,
`tool/ci.sh`, `flutter3d_testing`, 33 conformance checks, a sample agent
scenario in CI. Checked: `main` is red for three reasons from the HANDOFF —
a phase-0 blocker. A detector probe: `brush`, `bone`, `face`, `bevel`,
`loop`, `manifold` pass; `dashed`, `reload`, `spike`, `oneWay`, `lap`,
`boss`, `magazine` don't. The scanner's numeral lists stop at twenty-eight
(packages) and forty-five (scenes).

Its own fixture said nothing downstream decoded a pixel and that "this case never exports"; the second half was not true. Godot said `IHDR: CRC error`, then `Couldn't load image index '0' with its given mimetype: image/png`, and dropped the texture. `fmt-11`'s validator and `compareModelDocuments` had both been green on that file for as long as it existed, because neither one is a decoder that is not ours. The fixture writes a real 64×64 glaze through `encodeCompressedPng` now. **And what the comparison can honestly hold two readers to, measured rather than assumed:** surfaces, triangles and material names agree exactly on all seven committed GLBs; vertex counts do *not*, in either direction (Godot's importer merges `table.glb`'s legs 78 → 76 and splits `case1.glb`'s teapot 6768 → 7567), and node counts do not either (it folds a skin's joints into one `Skeleton3D`: 96 → 82, 45 → 9) — so neither is compared. Bounds are, and they are the one geometric check here: Godot's mesh AABB contains ours on every surface of every fixture (worst violation 1.9e-9), and on an unskinned surface the two agree to 2.4e-7. | qa-20n *(added 2026-09-18, asked for by an application built on this engine)* | **A run on a real phone, rather than a build of one — and a written policy for the Flutter pin.** Both platforms are *compiled* on every push, which `qa-17` is the row for: the `android` job builds four demos as a matrix with `fail-fast: false`, and the `macos` job runs `flutter build ios --no-codesign` four times. **Nothing runs.** No step starts an application on a device and reads anything back, so "it works on an iPhone" is a sentence this repository cannot currently support, and the Impeller half of the golden set — the forty-four scenes that need a real GPU — is still run by hand from `packages/flutter3d/tool/golden.sh`. That gap is honest and it is also the only one of these four requests that cannot be answered by reading the tree. The second half is the part nobody has written down. The Flutter version is pinned in three jobs at `3.47.0`; the Impeller bundle is gitignored and rebuilt by `impellerc` out of whichever Flutter is installed, so the *mechanism* for a version change already exists and the *policy* does not — what is rechecked when the pin moves, who finds out when a shader stops compiling under a new `impellerc`, and how far behind stable this is willing to sit. A consumer asking the question is asking about the policy, not the mechanism | ci + doc | M | 1 | qa-17 | one iPhone and one Android phone each run a named demo, with a frame time and a peak memory recorded in the HANDOFF against a date and a device; and a paragraph says what moving the Flutter pin requires and what it invalidates |

### 2.11 Publishing and product (`rel-`)

The release infrastructure is almost entirely automatic: `publish_check.sh`,
building the API reference by directory, `llms.txt` from NAV, `demos.sh`, CI
builds an unsigned iOS binary and a signed Android one. The 0.6.0 set, 27 of
28 on pub.dev; the names `flutter3d_mesh`, `flutter3d_model_core`,
`flutter3d_model_mcp`, `flutter3d_modeler` are free (checked 2026-09-09), as
are `flutter3d_geometry`, `flutter3d_formats`, `flutter3d_fbx`,
`flutter3d_cloth`. Friction points — manual lists; the site doesn't show a
fourth game, so "next to four games" rests on an unclosed ROADMAP item.

### 2.12 Footnotes for changed phases

Sizes didn't change. The phase changed for three items during synthesis
(¹–³) and five more by 2026-09-09 owner decisions (⁴–⁸), each with its own
reason:

¹ pro-job-01 — from phase 4 to 2: merged with doc-24 (phase 2), because a
task runner is already needed by phase 2's boolean modifiers and graph
baking (mat-11, mat-20), not only by phase 4.

² anim-26 — from phase 3 to 1: merged with fmt-07, because a glTF writer
with no skins silently drops an imported model's rig, and fmt itself puts
skins in phase 1 (⚠ against the ROADMAP, §7 item 7).

³ qa-11 — from phase 1 to 2: merged with view-14, whose default phase is 2
and phase 1 only by p0-06's outcome; the conformance check travels with the
contract change, not ahead of it.

⁴ ui-21 — from phase 2 to 1: decision 3 (phase 1 ships on macOS, web,
Android, and iOS), platform configuration is needed for the first version;
§7 #34 amended.

⁵ fmt-25 — from phase 3 to 2: decision 18 (a dedicated FBX reader), skins
and animation follow fmt-24 down the same track rather than waiting for the
character pipeline.

⁶ doc-11a-n — same phase (2), the "per §7 #36" condition dropped: decision
10, placing assets is unconditionally part of "Scene" mode.

⁷ fmt-26 — dropped: decision 18, no server-side conversion.

⁸ ui-34d — a new phase-1 item, conditional, whereas F11 [Е11] had put the
web worker in phase 2: decision 2, if p0-07/p0-08 show a freeze longer than
1 s.
### 2.13 An agent that can see the model (`mcp-`)

Aspect added 2026-09-10. `doc-19`…`doc-22` set up a server, a session of ten
verbs, and a tool table from `modelCommandNames` — and not one of the ten
verbs is visual. The agent edits the model blind: it knows
`ExportReadiness`'s numbers and never sees the silhouette. The only picture
in the plan is `render_snapshot` from `pro-rn-04`, phase 4, behind the
offline renderer.

**Why the picture can't be handed over today.** `flutter3d_model_mcp` is a
flat Dart package: a rule `tool/structure.dart` and `tool/flat_dart_check.sh`
both hold, with the reason recorded in its own pubspec — "`dart run` won't
resolve a package depending on the Flutter SDK, so one Flutter import in
this graph isn't a heavier process, it's a server that won't start." The
level editor already hit this wall: its own `screenshot` tool exists and
**refuses** (`editor_tools.dart:389`), because `GraphicsDevice.present`
returns a Flutter widget.

**The untangling is cheaper than it sounds, and this is a finding from
investigation, not a hope.** In `packages/flutter3d/lib/`, Flutter is named
in **four files**, and all four are about loading assets: `asset_source.dart`
and `gltf_resolvers.dart` (`rootBundle`), `model_loader.dart` (`kIsWeb`),
`texture_upload.dart` (`dart:ui` for decoding). `Renderer`, `Scene`,
`CameraNode`, `DeviceMesh`, `RenderView`, and the whole pass graph don't name
Flutter. Same story in `flutter3d_cpu`: drawing is pure, Flutter is only
needed by `present()` and image decoding; `encodePng` is **already** pure
Dart. The obstacle is three points, not something woven throughout.

**The path from a project to a picture already exists.**
`ModelerStage.fromProject` → `renderFrame` → `encodePng` is exactly what
`frame_test.dart` already does, drawing 160×100 frames with no GPU. What's
missing is making it reachable from a process with no Flutter.

This round's decisions: the consumer is an agent working alongside a human;
the server lives in both homes (headless by default, GUI behind a flag);
transport — stdio for headless, local HTTP for GUI; the agent gets every
command plus composite recipes; addressing — numeric ids, as internally;
history is shared, but the agent only undoes its own; a journal underneath
it all.

| id | what | package | size | phase | depends | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| mcp-01n | **the contract**: `GraphicsDevice.present() → Widget` leaves `flutter3d_hardware` for a separate `DevicePresenter`, implemented by a Flutter wrapper sitting next to each backend. The hardware layer stops naming Flutter — the same way it already doesn't name a graphics API | hw + 4 backends + conformance | L | 1 | — | `flutter3d_hardware` and `flutter3d_conformance` are in `flatDartPackages`; 33 conformance checks are green on all four backends; a new scanner rule "the hardware layer names no Flutter". `present()` is gone from `GraphicsDevice` (`presentFrame` lives in `flutter3d_app`'s own conditional halves instead — a prior session's own work, confirmed by reading the current tree rather than assumed from this row), and both `flutter3d_hardware` and `flutter3d_conformance` are in `flatDartPackages` today, closing two of three acceptance clauses literally. **Closed 2026-09-17 on the third**, which had been argued around rather than built: the ban did exist, but as the back half of a rule called `the hardware layer names no graphics API`, so a `dart:ui` import was reported under the name of the one thing it is not, and a reader went looking for a `flutter_gpu` that was not there. `the hardware layer names no Flutter` is its own rule now (`_hardwareNamesNoFlutter`, `tool/structure/rules.dart`); both halves read the same files through `_hardwareFiles`, which is also where the "a scan over no files proves nothing" finding lives so neither half can go quietly empty. | mcp-08n ⚠ | Modes for the agent: `material / wireframe / normals / selection`. The agent sees flipped normals and n-gons with its own eyes, not only as a number in a report. Closed 2026-09-14 against this row's own acceptance, three modes of four: `render`'s and `renderSheet`'s own new `mode` argument (`render_tool.dart`) reads `material` (default), `normals` (`RenderShading.normals`), or `selection` (the session's own current `select`ion, tinted, rather than an id list a caller repeats). `packages/flutter3d_cpu/test/render_modes_test.dart` builds an inverted shell with `EditMesh.cuboid()..flipNormals()` and checks `normals` differs from `material` on more than 20% of the frame's own pixels, not merely `> 0` the way `mcp-05n`'s own test settled for. **The fourth mode landed 2026-09-17, and it did not need `view-07` after all.** The gap had been recorded three times — here, in `RenderShading`'s own doc comment and in `render_tool.dart` — each time as "`wireframe` waits on `view-07`'s edge-drawing": `MeshData.edgeIndices()`, `DeviceMesh.upload(withEdges:)`, a `MeshWireVertex` stage and a line topology in six fragment stages across four backends, which is a phase-2 row and is still the right answer for the *live viewport*. It is not the only way to draw a wire. `wire_overlay.dart` turns every polygon edge into a three-sided prism — six triangles an edge, geometry no backend needs a new topology for — and hangs it as a child of the object's own node, so it carries the transform without a second world matrix being computed. That trade is explicit in the file: it costs triangles, which is right for a frame an agent asks for once and wrong for a viewport drawing sixty a second. **The edges are the document's, which is the whole point of the mode and the one thing that could have been quietly wrong.** `EditMesh` holds real n-gons; the renderer sees them fanned. A wireframe built from drawn triangles shows a cube as 18 edges — six quads with a diagonal across each — and a hexagon as a fan, so an agent told "this object has n-gons" would be looking at a mesh that appears to have none. `polygonEdgesOf` walks half-edges face by face and answers **12** for a cube; the test names 18 as the mutation. The surface under the wires goes flat and pale and the frame drops tonemapping, for the reason the weights gradient does: both colours are named by hand, and a lit surface shades half the model to where a near-black wire on it is one indistinguishable dark shape. Five checks in `render_modes_test.dart` (the edge count, the triangle count, an empty mesh answering null rather than an empty draw call, the picture changing *and* a wire actually landing dark on it, and an `ImportedGeometry` object getting the flat surface and no wires because it has no topology to read), plus one at the tool boundary in `render_tool_test.dart` — the half a typo in one schema string would break while every measurement stayed green | mcp | S | 1 | mcp-06n, view-13 | `normals` on an inverted shell differs from `material` by more than 20% of pixels — **done, and all four modes are reachable now: the fourth was measured rather than waited for** |

### 2.14 Game graphics (`gfx-`)

Aspect added 2026-09-10. These are **engine changes**, not editor ones: each
goes into §6 and is checked by conformance, a golden frame, or a round trip,
like any engine change. They're here because the editor is what made these
gaps visible: it assembles an asset, and the game shows it.

**This track runs alongside the modeler's twenty tasks** and is numbered
with its own phases G1–G3, so as not to pretend to be part of the §5
milestones.

**Priority is set by observation, not taste.** The target budget is 60
frames on a MacBook at 1440p; a typical scene is one close-up hero, one or
two characters on screen; the engine needs to carry both a stylized look and
a physical one. Four things are named as seen with one's own eyes: light
popping, jagged edges, distant objects with no shadows, aliasing on the
floor and on far textures. The list starts with them.

**Order inside G2 is set by one finding.** Filling the surface buffer turns
off MSAA for the whole scene, so SSAO and reflections today cost the game
its anti-aliasing — not a missing feature, a mutual exclusion between two
existing ones. FXAA removes it and unlocks both "jagged edges" and "contact
shadows" at once, so it comes first.

**What isn't in this track, and why.** A sort key, occlusion culling,
streaming, and geometry compression are about scene scale, and "one close-up
hero" on desktop doesn't hit that wall; the web matters to the modeler, not
to games. Vertex-shader skinning — with one or two characters, the CPU keeps
up. Decals, water, terrain, and atmospheric scattering are genre-specific,
and with no game that needs them there's nothing to optimize. Inverse
kinematics moved into the `anim-` aspect: it's needed for posing during
auto-rigging, and in a game the pose comes from a clip. Everything under the
ROADMAP's "Not doing" — TAA, motion blur, an ECS renderer, terrain clipmaps,
node-graph materials, OIT beyond alpha hashing, quality presets — isn't
proposed again.

**Two of those have since been written down anyway, and the difference is who
asked.** `gfx-84n` holds the material language and `gfx-87n` holds terrain, both
declined in the ROADMAP and both in G5. Nothing above is being reversed: a
declined item stops being proposed by *us*, and neither row argues for doing it.
What changed is that somebody building on the engine asked for them by name, and
an ask that lands as an absence teaches the asker that the plan is not where
decisions live. So the decline gets a row with the reasoning and the cost on it,
which is the same thing G5 was created for one paragraph further down.

**G4 and G5 are driven by a comparison rather than by observation, and that
is a different thing.** G1 to G3 were ordered by four defects somebody saw with
their own eyes. `doc/pipeline-comparison.md` measured the whole pipeline
against flutter_scene at a pinned commit and came back 68 claims behind against
35 ahead, 48 to 12 among the load-bearing ones.

**The first rows cost no new machinery.** They are things we already compute
and then throw away, or walk twice, or recompute per primitive. That is the
useful shape of the finding: our worst per-frame costs are not missing
features, they are existing work being discarded, and every one is cheaper than
the feature rows that follow. `gfx-61n` is the clearest case, where the exact
world box is computed on the culling path and dropped in favour of the sphere
derived from it in the same function call.

**G4 is the cycle; G5 exists so the question has an answer attached.** The
owner's decision, 2026-09-18: fix what is broken and lift the two ceilings, and
do not chase feature breadth, because verification is where this engine is
ahead and a second copy of theirs would be worse at both. G5 holds the breadth
rows anyway, ordered after the ceilings, so that a decision to decline one is a
row somebody can read rather than an absence somebody has to notice. Both
ceilings are in, `gfx-75n` first.

**That decision was reversed on 2026-09-18, by the same owner, after G4 closed
17/17.** G5 is now the cycle as well, worked in number order, and the three rows
proposed for declining are worked rather than declined. The paragraph above is
kept as written instead of being edited into agreement, because what it records
is a decision that was true when it was made and the reasoning behind it has not
been shown wrong — verification is still where this engine is ahead. What changed
is the state it was reasoning about: it was made with two ceilings unlifted and a
cycle unfinished, and neither is true now.

**Numbers 46 to 49 and 52 to 57 stay empty.** They were reserved by the
postprocessing survey and never filled, and a number that once meant nothing
should not quietly start meaning something. G4 begins at `gfx-60n`.

| id | what | package | size | phase | depends | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| gfx-01n | **A frame profiler**: per-pass counters (time, draw calls, triangles, pipeline switches) in `FrameResult`, a panel over the viewport, a draw-call baseline in CI. First item by owner decision: with no numbers, any next item is an impression, not a result. **Built 2026-09-17, and it found three wrong numbers on its first run.** `FramePass` (`frame_result.dart`) is a record per graph node — name, time, draws, triangles, pipeline switches — filled by differencing `FramePassState` either side of each `node.execute`, which asks the question from outside rather than threading a node identity down into every drawing path. `MetricsOverlay` lists them under its four totals and shows nothing when the list is empty; `flutter3d_sim_mcp`'s own `passes` tool hands an agent the same numbers. The baseline is `packages/flutter3d/test/frame_baseline_test.dart`: three parity fixtures with their draw counts written down exactly — not a budget with slack in it, since a baseline that tolerates nine extra draws is already wrong by nine when the tenth arrives — and a failure prints the pass that grew. **The three wrong numbers:** the shadow map and the whole bloom ladder were drawing and counting nothing, so `FrameResult.drawCalls` had never included either — the directional fixture read 4 and is 13, bloom read 3 and is 12; `frame_triangle_count_test.dart`'s "+1 for the procedural sky" was the composite's full-screen triangle, with the sky drawing nothing in those scenes; and `view-22`'s own guard, written earlier the same day, had called that same composite draw "the floor". All three were two numbers that happened to agree, which reads exactly like one number that is right | engine + qa | M | G1 | — | a regression of +10 draw calls breaks the build; the panel shows four passes separately |
| gfx-02n | **An anisotropy measurement.** `RenderSettings.anisotropy` defaults to 1, and no demo raises it; the level-editor bridge gives bricks `min(8, maxAnisotropy)`, an imported model gets 1. Capture one frame at 1 and at 8 on a scene with a floor receding into the distance, and decide whether to change the default. **Measured 2026-09-17, and taking the measurement meant building the thing first.** The only backend this machine renders through answered `maxAnisotropy` of one — its own comment said so and named `anisotropic-floor` as the scene where the software set is allowed to differ from the hardware ones by exactly that — so there was nothing to capture at eight. `BoundTexture.sample` now takes the taps: several along the long axis of the footprint, each at the level the *short* axis asks for, and `CpuDevice.maxAnisotropy` moved to sixteen with them, because a backend that promises taps it does not take is the worse of the two failures. **A sampler that asked for one is untouched** — not close, the same bytes, because the new path is not reached — which is what let this land on a backend forty-four golden scenes are recorded on with all 1237 of `flutter3d`'s own tests green and no reference moved. **Two things the fixture found.** The engine's own footprint was already collapsed to a per-axis maximum before the sampler saw it, which throws away the one number anisotropy needs — the *ratio* between the footprint's sides — so the first run measured zero differing pixels; `uvFootprint` now carries both screen derivatives as well, read only when a sampler asked for taps. And the software rasteriser derives them *per triangle* rather than per fragment, so a floor of two triangles has one footprint for the whole of itself: the fixture's plane is sixty-four segments square, which is a property of this backend written down rather than a trick for the picture. **The measurement: 3165 of 14400 pixels differ — 22% of the frame — and the contrast between neighbouring pixels goes from 5.36 to 9.20, so 1.7 times as much detail survives.** **The decision: yes, raise it** — that is a large, visible improvement for a setting nothing currently turns on, and the flip is `gfx-07n`'s own row, which says what it costs: every golden frame recaptured in one commit, on four backends, three of which this machine cannot record | engine | S | G1 | gfx-01n | two frames and the number of differing pixels — **3165 of 14400**, in `anisotropy_test.dart`; the decision is recorded in the plan — **raise it, in `gfx-07n`, when the golden sets can be recaptured together** |
| gfx-03n | **A distant-shadow measurement.** Three options — raise `viewDistance` with a recomputed split, a fourth cascade, a separate, rarely-updated far map — on one scene, with a frame cost from gfx-01n. **Measured 2026-09-17, and the first finding is that the row asks to compare three options of which the engine has one.** `ShadowSettings.cascades` is one to three — the atlas is `resolution × cascades` wide and the shader walks that many — so a fourth is a wider atlas and a longer loop in six fragment stages across four backends, not a number anybody can pass. And there is no static-bake field at all: the row's third option is *absent* rather than unmeasured. `distant_shadow_test.dart` says both as checks rather than as a comment, so a reader finds it out where they would look. **What the option that exists costs: nothing.** Fitting the cascades further out draws no more — nine draws at sixty metres, nine at two hundred and forty — because the same casters go into the same three maps and only the matrices differ. So reaching further is not a time cost at all; the whole trade is resolution, which is what `viewDistance`'s own doc comment already said and what this turns into numbers. **The wall clock is deliberately not asserted**: the pass reports its own microseconds and they came out 26597, 18001 and 17195 for sixty, a hundred and twenty and two hundred and forty — *decreasing*, which a longer fit cannot cause. That is the measurement warming up, and it is the honest limit of what this machine can say about a frame cost, the same limit `gfx-01n`'s profiler has on a backend with no GPU behind it | engine | S | G1 | gfx-01n | three frames and three costs — **one option measured on three distances, and the other two priced as unbuilt rather than pretended at**; the chosen option is named in gfx-06n — **`viewDistance`, because it is free in the shadow pass and the alternatives are a shader change on four backends and a feature that does not exist** |
| gfx-04n | **FXAA in the pass graph.** Removes the mutual exclusion: today a game chooses between shadows in the corners and smooth edges, because the surface buffer turns off MSAA for the whole scene. **Built 2026-09-17.** `fxaa.frag` with `FxaaShader` mirroring it in the software rasteriser, `AntiAliasSettings` (`render_settings.dart`), and `_FxaaNode` registered after the composite — registration order *is* the version chain, so the node reads the picture the composite wrote and produces the next one without anything being threaded or branched on outside `renderer.dart`. The one thing that runs the other way round from every other post pass: the composite draws into a pooled texture and this draws into the renderer's own finished-frame one, because the texture that leaves the frame has to be the renderer's — a pooled one would go back to the pool while the compositor was still sampling it. Off by default, and the renderer does not turn it on behind a caller's back even where MSAA has just been taken away. **The first version of the blend arithmetic was wrong in a way that only a measurement catches**: it scaled the step by how far a pixel sat from the *end* of the local range, which is exactly zero on a hard black-to-white edge — the pass ran, cost its draw, and changed nothing. It scales by the distance from the neighbourhood average now, squared, so a faint gradient is left alone and a real edge gets the whole step. `antialias_test.dart` counts the intermediate pixels along a diagonal, which is a number that moves for the right reason where a golden of a lit sphere would not, and its last test is the row in one sentence: occlusion on, and the edges smooth anyway | engine + shaders | M | G2 | gfx-01n | SSAO is on and edges are smooth; frame `ao-with-aa` across three sets; a pass cost from the profiler |
