A *time* rather than a pose, because the reference is a frame of the clip in almost every case — the convention every tool exports is that frame zero is at rest — and sampling the clip there costs nothing and needs no second structure; a caller whose reference is elsewhere builds a one-frame clip and points at it. **Null means the clip is not additive**, which is what every clip read from a file has, so a layer asking to add over an ordinary clip gets the override path rather than a delta measured from a pose nobody declared: guessing at frame zero would work for most exports and silently ruin the rest. Each path adds in whatever way undoes itself at a delta of nothing — a difference for a translation, a *ratio* for a scale (a swell authored at scale one must swell a chest at scale two by the same proportion), `base · delta` for a rotation so a lean authored on a standing chest still reads as a lean on a turned one, and the same subtraction for morph weights, where a blink over a half-open eye adds the change it makes rather than the eyelid twice. The rotation delta is scaled as an *angle*, not component-wise — and the fixture for that uses a quarter weight rather than a half, because a renormalised component scale is exactly the slerp at a half by symmetry and would have passed against the mutation it exists to catch (22.50° against 21.60° at a quarter, measured). A joint the base says nothing about is the one place where adding and overriding are the same answer, and the code path says why: the joint sits at what the model authored, which is what a reference frame *is*, so reference · delta is the sample already being written | engine | M | G3 | — | breathing over walking; zero changes to existing clips; `animation_mask_test` green |

Two things the fixture found. The early local-box rejection was against the *bind pose's* box and threw away exactly the rays this exists to catch — a ray through a raised arm misses the box the arm was exported in — so `PosedMesh` gathers the posed box while it poses, at one comparison a vertex. And the weights are renormalised here where the shader divides by nothing: a vertex whose four weights sum to 0.9 would otherwise be posed a tenth of the way back towards the origin, which reads as a hole in the silhouette exactly where a click lands. Morphing still answers about the base shape, and that is now said as a separate decision with its own reason rather than folded in with this one | engine | M | G3 | mesh-20 | a hit on a raised arm registers; a miss past it doesn't; the query cost is measured |

Two paths, because the existing selection short-circuits: a scene inside eight lights packs the matching ones unranked (`gatherMatchingFrom`), a scene that overflows filters before it scores (`gatherNearFrom`'s new `channels`), and a frame where nothing is channelled takes the untouched fast path and shares the frame's own arrays — `LightBuffer.anyChannelled` is what the renderer asks, so "zero changes to frames with no channels" is a thing a test checks rather than a hope. The acceptance is written as an *equality*: an excluded object is lit exactly as it is with the lamp shining on nothing, because a ratio would also pass for a light that leaked a little and leaking a little is the failure this is for. **The fixture caught one of mine**: with bloom on, the lit quad's glow spreads across the frame and lifts the excluded half, which read as a leaking channel until it was measured — the test turns bloom off and says why | engine | S | G3 | gfx-05n | an object outside the channel gets no contribution; zero changes to frames with no channels |
| gfx-13n | **Physical light units**: lumens and candela, converted into today's dimensionless intensity. **Built 2026-09-17** as `Photometric` (`light_node.dart`) — `fromLumens`/`fromCandela`/`fromLux` and their inverses, with `referenceIlluminance` the one number the whole exchange rate hangs off. It is fixed at the point this row's own acceptance names: `800 / 4π` = 63.66 lux, which is what makes **an 800-lumen point lamp come out at intensity 1.0** — eight hundred lumens being the bulb that replaced the sixty-watt incandescent, and one being what every light in this engine has defaulted to. Everything else follows by arithmetic: a point lamp spreads its flux over the sphere, a spot over `2π(1 − cos θ)` of its cone (so the same bulb through a 45° spot is 6.83× as intense), a directional light is rated in lux and has no falloff to spread anything over. **Nothing is applied automatically** — these are functions a caller reaches for, not a mode the renderer enters, so every hand-tuned scene keeps the numbers it was tuned with and `LightNode.intensity` stays dimensionless. The cone's solid angle is clamped away from nothing, because a cone of no width would make one lumen infinitely bright. **The fixture saturated on its first run and the second assertion is what caught it**: at the anchor distance of a metre an 800-lumen lamp blows the wall to 255, so the equality read as agreement when it was really two clipped whites — the lamp moved to five metres, and "a brighter bulb is a brighter wall" is there so an equality can never pass alone | engine | M | G3 | gfx-05n | an 800-lumen lamp gives the same illuminance as today's tuned number — held both as arithmetic and as pixels in `photometric_test.dart`, and a hemisphere-instead-of-sphere mutation is caught by both |
| gfx-14n | **`KHR_lights_punctual`** in the loader and writer: light from glTF is entirely lost today | formats | S | G3 | fmt-06 | round trip: light from Blender opens and exports back |
| gfx-15n | **Soft disc shadows with five taps**: promised in the ROADMAP, today it's a 3×3 PCF with an edge that's always equally hard. **Built 2026-09-17.** `ShadowSettings.directionalLightRadius` above zero swaps the fixed nine-tap filter for a blocker search on five points of a disc followed by a five-tap filter whose radius comes from how far the occluder turned out to be — so a box resting on a floor keeps a hard edge and the same box a metre up spreads. Zero is the kernel this renderer has always had, which is what keeps every recorded golden where it is, and it is the default. Written in `shadow.glsl` and mirrored in `cpu_shaders_shadow_directional.dart`, carried to all four backends. The parameter rides in `ambient_ground.w` — named in both files, because a reader arriving at *ambient* has every right to be surprised: `frame_params.w` was the slot reserved for a frame-wide number and the environment's level count took it, and appending to a block six shaders share moves offsets four backends agree on. **Two things the measurement corrected.** The radius was scaled by a constant I had picked to make the arithmetic scene-independent, and it is not: the map stores linear depth over the cascade's own volume, so the unit is texels of penumbra per unit of stored depth and it is scene-dependent in exactly the way `AmbientOcclusionSettings.radius` already documents itself to be — the constant went and the doc says what the number means, with a measured example. And the blocker search radius was proportional to the softness, which at working values reached hundreds of texels and would have found occluders belonging to another part of the scene; it is bounded now. `soft_shadow_test.dart` counts the part-lit pixels at three heights and holds the trend, and separately holds that the fixed kernel does *not* widen the same way — the difference being the whole row | shaders + engine | S | G3 | — | frame `soft-shadow`; the penumbra is wider for a farther occluder |
| gfx-16n | **Alpha hashing**: foliage and nets today are either hard-cut by a threshold or need sorting. **Built 2026-09-17** as `MaterialAlphaMode.hashed`, the one mode in that enum glTF has no word for: a fragment is kept or dropped against noise rather than a constant, so a leaf texture at 40% keeps 40% of its *pixels* — in the opaque half, writing depth, needing no sort. **Hashed on world position rather than on the screen**, which is a line longer and the difference between a branch that keeps its speckle as it moves and one that sparkles as it passes through a fixed pattern; `alpha_hash_test.dart` separates the two by moving the quad and the camera together, so the surface covers the same pixels and a screen-space hash would have drawn the identical frame. The sentinel rides in `material2.x` below -1.5 — -1 already meant "not masked" and anything more negative was free, where a second number would have been a member added to a block six shaders share. Picking treats hashed as masked at a fixed half, because a click is one pixel and answering it with noise would make selecting a leaf a coin toss. The price is stated in the enum rather than discovered: without temporal accumulation the noise is visible wherever the frame is not averaged down, so it suits a supersampled render or a distant canopy better than a leaf held up to the camera | shaders + engine | S | G3 | — | frame `foliage`; zero changes in opaque scenes |
| gfx-17n | **LUT grading and a filmic curve**: `LookSettings` exists, no table does. **The curve half is built 2026-09-17**, the table half is not. `TonemapCurve` (`render_settings.dart`) is a final class with const instances — `neutral`, `aces`, `agx`, `reinhard` — whose `code` goes into `CompositeInfo.params.z`, the slot that used to hold a flag; numbering the default 1 is what makes `> 0.5` and `int(z + 0.5)` agree about every scene already recorded, and `tonemap_curve_test.dart` holds `neutral` to `tonemapNeutral` value for value so thirty goldens cannot move under it. Written in `composite.frag` and mirrored in `cpu_shaders_color.dart`, carried to all four backends through `build_shaders.sh`, the WebGL generator and glslang + naga. **Three of my own claims about these curves were wrong until they were measured**: the Narkowicz ACES fit *lifts* midtones rather than darkening them (0.18 → 0.267 against the neutral curve's 0.140), AgX puts middle grey at middle display (0.497) which is a bigger change than a look, and the extended Reinhard keeps climbing past its own white point — it mapped 12 to 1.6 — so it clamps now. The numbers are written into the test rather than into prose. The LUT itself is `gfx-18n`, built the same day, which is what closes this row's own acceptance | engine | S | G3 | — | a neutral LUT doesn't change the frame by even a pixel |
| gfx-18n *(added 2026-09-17, splitting gfx-17n)* | **The LUT half of `gfx-17n`**: a colour table sampled in the composite after the tone curve, as a 2D strip of N slices of N×N, with a strength and an identity table bound when nobody set one. Its own row because the curve half is arithmetic in a shader already written and this one adds a sampler to a uniform block four backends read, plus a texture the engine has to build, plus the question the row's own acceptance asks and the curve half cannot answer: whether an identity table is *exactly* a no-op or merely nearly one, which depends on the texture format and the filtering and has to be measured rather than assumed. **Built 2026-09-17.** `LookSettings.lut`/`lutStrength` and `buildIdentityLut` (`render_settings.dart`); `SampleLut` in `composite.frag` with `_sampleLut` mirroring it in `cpu_shaders_post.dart`; the strength and the table's N ride in `ao_texel.z`/`.w`, two components that were documented as unused, so the uniform block four backends read keeps its layout. The table sits after the grade and before the vignette — a LUT is somebody's finished look, so it should see the contrast and saturation decisions and not the lens. **The measured answer to the row's own question: it is exact, and not because the table is.** An eight-bit entry is up to half a step off and the sampler interpolates between entries that are each wrong; what rescues it is the other end, where the composite encodes to eight bits of sRGB and half a step rounds back to the byte it started from. That is a property of the pair rather than of the table, and a float target would have to be measured again — said in the test rather than only here. Mitigated only by every effect that reads the buffer being off by default. **Built 2026-09-18** as `maxColorAttachments` on `GraphicsDevice`, answered by every backend: WebGL2 queries its draw-buffer limit, WebGPU states the specification's floor, the rasteriser takes it as a parameter so the no-MRT path can be run with pixels at the end of it, and the Impeller backend **infers** from `supportsRenderToMip` — written down rather than hidden, because flutter_gpu publishes no MRT capability and the probe that would settle it is the call that ends the process. `checkAttachmentLimit` throws on the way into `beginRenderPass` in every backend, because a refusal one backend makes and another does not is worse than none | hardware + engine | M | G3 | — | a pass past the limit throws rather than aborting, and every pass that reads the surface buffer is culled and reported as unsupported |

The mean is 1.86 against a budget of 1, and the residue is honest rather than fixable: additive noise on pure black can only lift, because there is nothing below zero, so the reference needs re-recording either way. | gfx-60n *(added 2026-09-18 from doc/pipeline-comparison.md)* | **Alpha-masked shadow casters.** `shadow_depth.frag` ended in `frag_color = vec4(gl_FragCoord.z, 0.0, 0.0, 1.0)` unconditionally and `shadow_distance.frag` wrote its clamped distance the same way, with no alpha, cutoff or mask anywhere in the shadow pass, so every foliage card, fence, grate and chain-link cast the shadow of its whole quad. **Built 2026-09-18** as `ShadowDepthMasked` and `ShadowDistanceMasked`: two stages rather than a branch, so a caster that is not cut out keeps the pipeline it has always had, with no sampler in it and no texture bound per draw, which is also why the forty-four goldens could not move. Both shadow paths take it, and the point light is where the omission showed worst: a cube face is a ninety-degree frustum with the caster close to it, so a quad's slab fills far more of the tile than it would in a cascade. **Two things the test found.** A light straight overhead puts the shadow underneath the card, where the camera cannot see it, so the fixture is lit at an angle. And the first draft asserted that nothing anywhere gets darker, which a soft filter does not support: PCSS sizes its penumbra from the blockers it finds, so removing one re-estimates the boundary. | gfx-62n *(added 2026-09-18 from doc/pipeline-comparison.md)* | **The BVH at every scene size, and a refit instead of a rebuild.** `RenderList.bvhThreshold = 2048`: below that we scan linearly, and above it we rebuild whenever the stamp changed, because `SceneBvh` has no refit. Theirs uses a Morton-curve BVH over world AABBs for every cull at every size and separates structural dirt, an item added or removed, from an item that merely moved, so a scene where things only move refits in one forward pass. Ours pays a full rebuild because one node turned. **Built 2026-09-18**, and the benchmark written to settle the threshold found something the row had not: the refit was the easy half. `SceneBvh.refresh` now refits when the mesh count is unchanged — the partition is over index positions rather than identities, so any assignment of `0..n-1` to leaves is a valid tree and a refit answers correctly even for a frame that swapped what an index refers to — and rebuilds when the summed node area has doubled, which is the standard proxy for a tree that has stopped separating anything. Measured: 23 ms down to 0.85 ms on 50 000 meshes, 14× to 44× across the sizes. **The thing the row missed is that the tree could not win at any size, and the threshold was not why.** `RenderList` repacked every mesh's bounding sphere each frame, because packing was how it discovered whether anything had moved; that pack is a full pass with three version-checked getters apiece and costs 3.3 ms on 50 000 meshes — the entire price of the linear cull it was meant to replace. So `SceneNode.changeEpoch` is the row's real content: the counter that hands out version stamps now also advances when dirt is *introduced*, not only when a matrix is recomputed, and a reader holding a previous value knows in one comparison that nothing was moved, reparented, added or removed. The comparison priced our write side against theirs and called it a win; the refuter priced the reads, and a frame is made of reads. **Built 2026-09-18** on the epoch `gfx-62n` introduced, which had to be split first: the counter that hands out version stamps advances when a matrix is recomputed too, and what a reader needs is a counter that moves only when something is *touched*. `_dirtyEpoch` is that, and `worldMatrix` now returns on one integer comparison when the node verified itself at the current one — a node that did has an ancestor chain nobody has touched since. `visibleInHierarchy` is cached the same way, and `visible` became a setter so that hiding a branch reaches everything under it; it is guarded on the value, because `LodGroup` writes every level's flag every frame to pick one. **The limit is stated as a measurement rather than as a caveat**: the epoch is global, so it cannot tell one subtree's change from another's, and a move anywhere makes every node walk once more and then stop. `world_transform_cache_test.dart` holds that number — a hundred leaves plus two roots is 102 walks after a move and 0 on every read after — which is the right way round, because the walk is per read and the change is per move. **The figure that shows what it was worth is one the benchmark printed by accident.** `Skeleton.update` on 64 joints with a new pose each time read 16.4 us before today; `gfx-64n`'s pose-stamp guard pushed it to 27.0, because the stamp walks every joint's `worldVersion` and that walked to the root; with this row it is **4.7 us**, three and a half times better than where the day started | engine | M | G4 | gfx-01n | reading `worldMatrix` on a node whose ancestors have not moved touches no ancestor, counted, and the parity fixtures are unchanged |

| gfx-69n *(added 2026-09-18 from doc/pipeline-comparison.md)* | **The automated build compresses textures, and writes a mip chain.** `buildAssets` calls `convertOne` with no `textures:` argument, so it takes `TextureFamily.auto` and emits the PNG or JPEG that arrived, inside `.f3d`, every time; the `--textures bc` path that every discussion of our asset pipeline argues about is a hand-run flag the application path never reaches. And `_encodeOne` writes exactly one level, so a compressed texture has no chain, which is the one case where compression makes the picture worse because minification has nothing to fall back to. Two halves of one wrong default rather than two features. **The mip half landed 2026-09-18 and the default half did not, on purpose.** `_encodeOne` now builds a chain: the base, then each level halved by a box filter and encoded in the same format, stopping where the blocks stop — 64 gives five levels, 12 gives one, because 6 is not whole 4×4 blocks and cannot be encoded at all. A box filter rather than anything sharper, because a mip level is the value a bilinear tap would have found had it read four texels at once, and a sharper kernel is aliasing put back by hand. Averaged in the channel's stored values, matching what the hardware does between levels. What the reasoning missed is that a block format is guaranteed by the *platform*, not by a device: every desktop GPU samples BC, and ETC2 is required by OpenGL ES 3.0 and by Metal on every iOS device this engine runs on. So `familyForTargetOS` maps macOS, Windows and Linux to BC, Android and iOS to ETC2, and everything else — the web included — to `auto`, because a browser is whatever machine it runs on and `ap-09`'s answer there is both sets chosen at load time. The hook reads the target through `code_assets`, behind `buildCodeAssets`: `HookConfig.code` throws without a code configuration, and a build that cannot see its target must not invent one. Each pass carries its own reads, optional reads, writes and keeps by the frame graph's own names — which is what makes a capture walkable, since the pass that wrote a resource and the pass that read it are joined by a string — plus an image of everything it wrote or maintains. **The copy is taken before `endNode`**, which is where a version whose last reader has passed goes back to the pool: a capture taken after the frame would hold whatever the last pass to borrow that shape left there, attributed to whichever pass wrote it first. A resource with no image says why rather than going missing — tile memory, multisampled, a cube, a name nothing provided this frame — because a gap in a capture is worse than no capture, the reader assuming the pass wrote nothing. `FrameCapture.firstBlack` is the question in one call. **Exact on the software rasteriser**, whose readback copies before returning; on the hardware backends `readPixels` may resolve after the queue has moved on, and that is stated rather than papered over. Theirs has seven render lanes on every pull request, on merge and nightly. A comparison titled *what runs where* answered it with a backend count, and the reader's question is which platforms they can ship. What a build proves is the half that actually breaks — the CMake, the plugin registration, and every package in the graph compiling for a platform none of them had been asked about. What the cap costs is written down in `Renderer._drawLightsFor`: a surface large enough to touch many lights at once — a ground plane whose bounding sphere reaches every torch — scores them all at distance zero and keeps the eight brightest, and an instanced crowd spread across a map shares one list chosen for the whole batch; `RenderSettings.lightFadeBand` exists because the eight that reach an object change as it moves. **And the shape the work has to take was settled.** Raising `kMaxLights` is not the answer: the block is four `vec4` arrays uploaded per draw, so a bigger array is a bigger upload on every draw in every scene. Clustering needs a light index list the fragment stage can read — a texture, since the HAL has no storage buffers — plus a per-draw range, which is a new binding in `lib/surface.glsl` and a transcription in `flutter3d_cpu`, so five implementations. The eight-slot block stays as the path a scene with eight or fewer takes, which is what keeps the forty-four goldens byte for byte: the acceptance's second clause is met by construction rather than by comparison, the same way `gfx-60n`'s second stage kept them. **Built 2026-09-18.** `kExtraLights = 24` in `lib/surface.glsl`, a `sampler2D` holding every scene light four texels across, and a `LightListInfo` block of six `vec4`s of row numbers and six of fade scales, bound on every lit draw — a draw with no tail binds a count of nought and a one-by-one stand-in, because a declared sampler nobody binds is a native crash on Metal. Between them that is most of what a KTX2 file from a modern toolchain looks like, so a texture authored elsewhere does not open. Theirs decodes UASTC to RGBA8 or repacks the blocks straight to ASTC, and carries a pure-Dart zstd decoder for the supercompression. **The supercompression half is built 2026-09-18; the UASTC half is not, and the reason is a missing oracle rather than a missing afternoon.** `zstd.dart` is a Zstandard decompressor in Dart — frame and block headers, FSE, Huffman with both weight encodings, the four-stream literals layout, sequences and the three repeat offsets. Written here rather than reached for because this package depends on `flutter3d_hardware` and `vector_math` and nothing else, and every Dart zstd is a binding to the C library, which a web build cannot load; `inflate.dart` made the same call for DEFLATE one directory over. ZLIB supercompression came free beside it, since that inflate already reads the wrapper. Both wrap the ordinary level index rather than replacing it, so the loader changed by one call per level plus a check that a level unpacks to the length the index claims — a file where those disagree uploads a level that reads past the end of one of them. **Four bugs, and each was found by a fixture the real compressor wrote, not by re-reading the code.** The predefined match-length distribution had its tail in the wrong place and still summed to the table size, so it built cleanly and decoded a 2998-byte match as 751 — the sum is not the check, the shape is. The Huffman table was ranked by code length where the format ranks by weight, which are reverses of each other: a valid prefix code that fills exactly and decodes every byte wrong. The weight loop stopped when the bitstream was empty rather than when it could no longer afford the next state update, losing six symbols, because an update needing no bits is legal with no bits left. And the three repeat offsets were reset per block where they live for the whole frame, which every fixture under 128 KiB passed and the one past it failed at byte 131073. Thirty structured streams and sixty fuzzed ones across compression levels 1 to 19 now decode byte for byte. **UASTC stays refused, and the refusal message now says the block format is the problem rather than the compression.** Decoding it needs the mode tables, the BISE packing and the endpoint layout out of the Basis Universal specification, and `toktx`/`basisu` are not installable here — so it would be two thousand lines checked against nothing but my own reading of a specification, which is exactly the kind of work the four bugs above argue against: every one of them looked right and was caught only because an encoder somebody else wrote disagreed. A reference UASTC file is an `npm install` away, and so is the Basis Universal source the block layout would be transcribed from — the same position `gfx-82n` turned out to be in. The row stays partial because the work is not done, not because it cannot be; what was wrong was the reason, and the reason is the part somebody else would have relied on | core | M | G5 | — | a UASTC KTX2 and a zstd-supercompressed KTX2 both open and upload, and a BasisLZ file is unchanged |

A box the eye is inside projects to the whole viewport rather than to the corners that happened to be in front, since those are a strict subset and a ring drawn to them sits inside the thing it surrounds. And selection rides the platform's own three-valued flag rather than being written into the label, so a reader announces it in the person's own language instead of saying it twice in one of them. At a sixteen-millisecond budget that is about forty-six thousand splats on this machine, and the number is printed by the test rather than asserted, with a bound beside it that fails if the cost stops being linear. | gfx-81n *(added 2026-09-18 from doc/pipeline-comparison.md)* | **A runtime irradiance field.** Theirs is DDGI-shaped: octahedral probe tiles with a gutter, irradiance plus a luminance second moment, and depth-moment tiles carrying mean distance and mean square distance for the visibility test. | gfx-84n *(added 2026-09-18 from doc/pipeline-comparison.md)* | **The material language, not just the seam.** Theirs is a `.fmat` with a parser, an AST and an emitter: the author writes a material with typed parameters and a fragment body, and the toolchain generates the entry points, the variants and the binding metadata. `gfx-75n` takes the vertex seam, which is the ceiling; this is the authoring surface on top of it. **Proposed for declining and kept by owner decision**, and it is the row most likely to be answered by something other than itself, since the ROADMAP already declined node-graph materials once. **Built 2026-09-19.** `material_language/` holds a lexer, a parser that types every node as it builds it, a GLSL emitter and an evaluator; `MaterialProgramStage` in `flutter3d_testing` is the twenty lines that let the software backend draw one. **The tree is the thing both sides read, which is the point rather than an implementation detail**: a lighting model reaches four backends today as four texts, three of them one source translated and the fourth a person having read GLSL and written Dart, and that fourth is the one that can drift with nothing red. A material in this language has no transcription — `material_glsl.dart` writes GLSL from the tree and `material_eval.dart` evaluates the same tree, and `material_language_frame_test.dart` renders `Unlit` written in the language beside the hand-written `UnlitShader` at **zero tolerance**. **Parameters are compile-time constants and a variant is a fold**, which is what the row asks for when it says the toolchain generates the entry points and the variants. *Corrected 2026-09-19:* this row first said a parameter that changed per frame was impossible because it would need a uniform block per material; the engine already had one — `Material.parameters`, bound as `MaterialParams` — and the claim was made without looking. Runtime parameters would be members of that block; they are not written, and that is scope rather than a limit. **The binding metadata is read off the tree**, where `LightingModel`'s `uses…` flags are declared by hand under a comment saying reflection cannot answer the question — and `impellerc` was run on an emitted shader by hand to check: it kept `FogInfo`, `FragInfo` and `base_color_texture`, exactly what `describeMaterial` says. The generators are exercised for real — `translateGlsl` and `prepareStage` in `material_language_generators_test.dart` — and impellerc is not, because `build_shaders_test.dart` next door stubs it rather than depend on a machine having the SDK's artifact cache. What the language refuses is as deliberate as what it has: no uniform block, no sampler the engine does not bind, no loop, no `#define`, and no name that shadows a surface input. | gfx-86n *(added 2026-09-18, asked for by an application built on this engine)* | **A polyline of constant screen width, with joins.** Half of this is already here, and the missing half is the half a route line is made of. `MeshOverlay.ribbon` emits a band a given number of *logical* pixels across, turned to face the camera, in the `positionColor` layout the `DebugLine` pipeline reads — so it draws on all four backends today, and `overlay_ribbon_width_test.dart` measures the width in rendered pixels at two depths rather than trusting the vertices that went in. `pro-eng-07` closed on that. What is absent is everything that makes a sequence of segments one line. **Each call emits an independent quad**, so an elbow leaves a wedge of empty screen between two segments, which is the complaint the request puts first and is not a tuning matter but a different geometry: a miter, with a fallback where the angle is sharp enough that a miter would shoot off to infinity. **The colour is one `Vector4` per segment** while the batch stores a colour per vertex, so a gradient along the line — slope, speed, elevation — has somewhere to live and no way to get there. **`biasPixels` defaults to 12** and pushes the whole overlay toward the eye, which is right for an edge that has to sit *on* its own face and wrong for a line that has to disappear behind a ridge; the field is public and mutable, so zero is reachable, and nothing tests that a line at zero is actually occluded. **And the width is computed on the CPU** in `worldSize`, so the batch is rebuilt whenever the camera moves — for a track of tens of thousands of points, a rebuild per frame, which is the one of the four that decides the shape of the work. The expansion belongs in the vertex stage, where the camera costs nothing, and `gfx-75n` has just made a vertex stage something a material can supply from outside the engine — so this is a material rather than a new fixed path in the renderer, and the CPU ribbon stays for what it was written for. **Built 2026-09-19.** `polyline.vert` is `PolylineVertex` in the engine's bundle, compiled by `impellerc`, carried by both web generators, transcribed for the software backend, and linked against `Unlit` in the conformance table; `buildPolyline` writes each point twice into the standard layout repacked — the neighbours in the normal and tangent slots, the signed half width in tangent.w, the colour per point — and `Material.polyline` is `LightingModel.polyline` with the viewport as its own parameter. **Joins are mitres, held at four half widths**, the SVG default, so a hairpin narrows at its tip instead of spiking; a bevel would need a third vertex per point and this layout has two. **The viewport is the one thing no engine block carried to a vertex stage**, so `Material.parameters` — the channel an application already had for its own fragment stages — is now bound to a vertex stage the material brought as well; a resize writes two numbers. **And binding that exposed a latent `gfx-75n` fault**: `FrameInfo` was bound through the engine's `MeshVertex` handle even when the pipeline's vertex stage was the material's, which the software backend could not see and the reflecting backends got away with because a stage copying `mesh.vert` puts the block at the same index; it now binds through the stage the pipeline was built with. `polyline_test.dart` measures each clause in rendered pixels: two separate segments leave the outer corner of a 90° elbow empty and the joined line fills it; a red-to-blue line is red, then between, then blue; a box between the camera and the line hides its middle and not its ends; one uploaded buffer drawn from four and nine metres is the same width, and a resize is one list written. The forty-four goldens on all three checked backends did not move. Not built: bevel and round joins, end caps, picking a line (the id pass draws through the engine's stage, where a band has no area), and culling that knows the band is wider than its points | engine + shaders | M | G5 | gfx-75n | a polyline of N points draws as one band with no gap at a 90° elbow; the colour is read per point; a line behind a hill is hidden by it; and a camera move redraws without rebuilding the buffer |
| gfx-88n *(added 2026-09-18, found while starting gfx-83n)* | **The ASTC encoder writes blocks a real decoder refuses.** `astc4x4_encoder.dart` has always said so: its own doc comment records that the 11-bit block-mode field is "this port's own layout, not the specification's row-selection table", chosen because reproducing the real one "without a reference decoder to check against" risked a block that looks self-consistent and is not. That was an honest call and the premise behind it stopped being true the moment this machine was found to reach npm: ARM's own `astcenc` is a package away. Fed a file this encoder writes, it returns `(255, 0, 255)` for every block — ASTC's error colour — because an all-zero block mode is a reserved encoding rather than a 4×4 weight grid. **Nothing ships magenta today**, which bounds it: `texture_encode.dart` switches on `bc` and `etc2` and leaves anything else untouched, so no build can select ASTC; the function is exported, tested against a decoder written from the same misreading, and cannot do what its name says. **Built 2026-09-18, and the mode is not the one this row predicted.** The row said `0x242`, sixteen weight levels; what fits is `0x53` — single plane, 4×4 weight grid, **eight** levels — because sixteen levels over sixteen texels take 64 bits of weights and leave 49 for the block mode, the partition count, the endpoint mode and six endpoint values, which six eight-bit endpoints do not fit into. Eight levels cost 48 bits and leave the endpoints their full byte each. Three other things had to be right at once, and each of them alone gives a block that decodes to something: the weights sit from bit 127 downward with every byte's bits reversed; CEM 8 (LDR RGB Direct) reads the two endpoints in an order chosen by which of the two sums is larger, so a pair written the natural way comes back swapped; and the endpoint quantisation is the one left over after the weights, not one picked. `astc_conformance_test.dart` pins the bytes `astcenc` was actually handed and what it returned — worst channel error 7 and RMSE 2.7 over a 64×64 gradient, against solid magenta before. `gfx-83n` is unblocked: a device-agnostic cooked texture needs a 4×4 intermediate that something other than us can read | core | M | G5 | — | a block this encoder writes decodes in `astcenc` to the image it was given, within the error the quantisation allows |
| gfx-87n *(added 2026-09-18, asked for by an application built on this engine)* | **A heightfield as a rendering system: tiles without cracks, and levels of detail.** **Proposed for declining, and the decline is already written down.** `ROADMAP.md`'s *Not doing* says it in one line: "Terrain clipmaps. The heightfield above is collision and gameplay; making it a rendering system is a separate quarter." The heightfield that line points at is a *collision* shape — one of the two missing from the six the physics track commits to, so a strategy map and a racing circuit can be ground rather than boxes — and it is not built either. The request therefore asks for the second half of something whose first half has not started, across a boundary the owner drew deliberately. Recorded rather than left out, for the reason G5 exists: a decline somebody can read beats an absence somebody has to notice. What it would cost, stated so the size is not a guess: a grid per tile with a skirt or a stitched edge, because two neighbouring tiles at different levels open a crack at the seam and a skirt is the cheap answer; a level chosen per tile from the camera, with the hysteresis that stops a tile flipping between two levels on a slow approach; and a way to feed height and cover into a material, which is `gfx-84n`'s language rather than a shader written per terrain. Nothing here is novel, and all of it is a quarter. **Built 2026-09-19, by owner decision to do every G5 row, and smaller than the estimate because the premise above had gone stale.** The row said the collision heightfield "is not built either"; by the time it was picked up it was: `flutter3d_sim` has `Heightfield` and `HeightfieldGeometry`, and physics has `CollisionHeightfield`. What was missing was only the rendering half. `HeightfieldTiles` cuts a field into power-of-two tiles and builds any tile at any level, level `l` keeping every `2^l`-th sample, on the diagonal `Heightfield` fixed and with normals from the full-resolution field so a seam that the geometry closes does not reopen in the shading. **Skirts rather than stitching**: each edge is copied straight down and joined, so whatever gap a level difference opens has ground behind it, and a tile's triangles never depend on its neighbours' levels — a change of level rebuilds nothing. **The depth is measured, not guessed**: the largest distance any level's edge strays from the full-resolution line anywhere on the field, doubled because two neighbours can stray opposite ways. `TileLevelChooser` gives level `l` out to `nearest · 2^l` metres with a 15% band either side, so a camera creeping across a threshold changes level once and one hovering on it does not flip; `TerrainTiles` in `flutter3d_app` points a node per tile at the mesh its distance calls for and uploads a level the first time a tile needs it. `terrain_tiles_test.dart` measures a crack as red sky with white ground above and below it in the same column, and checks the measure three ways: none on a field at one level, some on a level 0 / level 2 seam without skirts, none on the same seam with them. Height and cover reach a material written in the language — `world.y` for bands, `sample` for a cover map — drawn through `MaterialProgramStage` with no shader written for the scene. Not built: geomorphing between levels (a change of level pops rather than blends), streaming tiles in and out of memory, and splatting more than the one cover texture the base colour slot carries | engine + shaders | L | G5 | gfx-84n | a heightfield draws with no crack at any seam between two levels; a tile's level follows the camera and does not flip on a slow approach; height and cover reach a material without a shader written for this scene |

### 2.15 The tutorial (`tut-`)

Aspect added 2026-09-14 by owner decision. `rel-09` promised one tutorial —
"an asset from import to engine in 15 minutes" — on the documentation site.
This is the bigger thing it grows into, and it moves house: the modeler's
tutorial is published on **models.pleion.dev** (`cloud/server`), where the
reader has an account, a cabinet to put the result into, and the modeler's
own web build at `/app/` to open it in. The documentation site keeps a card
pointing there (`rel-08` retargeted, not duplicated).

**Six cases, each a real job, not a feature tour.** A prop from a scan
(`teapot.stl` in millimetres → clean → material → GLB → cabinet); a vase from
a profile (lathe, mesh edits, the modal transform and snapping, modifiers, a
texture); a lit corner (two assets in one scene, lights, shadows, environment,
post, one GLB with two nodes); a character from a bare mesh
(`RobotExpressive.glb` without its skin → auto-rig → weights → pose and keys →
morphs → the game preview → GLB → a game template); borrowing a walk
(retargeting a clip); an agent beside you (`--mcp-port`, the first case done
by an agent, the journal, undoing only the agent's steps). Cases 1–3 are
possible after `ui-37d`/`mat-33d`/`mat-34d`; 4–6 need the animation screens.
The first draft of 1–3 is written **as soon as those land**, not at the end —
writing a case is how the gaps show up.

**Screenshots are driven, not clicked.** The app runs with `--mcp-port` and
the `mcp-16d` UI tools; `tool/tutorial/shoot.dart` reads the session file,
replays a case step by step, and captures the window with `screencapture -l`
(the window id from a ten-line `window_id.swift` over
`CGWindowListCopyWindowInfo` — no third-party tool). `MainFlutterWindow.swift`
learns a `--window=1440x900` argument so every picture is the same size.
Pictures of the result alone (no chrome) come from the headless `render`/
`renderSheet` tools and are reproducible in CI. Regenerating every picture is
one command.

**The tutorial is a test** (the `rel-09` shape): each case is a
`test/fixtures/tutorial/<case>.jsonl` journal in `flutter3d_model_mcp`, replayed
in CI against a reference `.f3dproj`, a GLB (`compareModelDocuments`) and a
frame. A step that cannot be written as document commands is a gap by
definition.

**The gap journal.** `doc/modeler-tutorial-gaps.md`: case · step · what was
expected · what is there · kind (feature / consistency / UX) · the plan row it
became. Known candidates before a single case is written: a dropped file is
decoded and then thrown away (`main.dart`'s `_handleDroppedFile`); `.gltf`
beside its `.bin` (`ui-36n`); the cabinet has no preview picture; a model
opened from the cabinet cannot be saved back; the status line has no vertex
or material count.

*(Checked during T6's close-out, 2026-09-15: `ui-36n` is done — a `.gltf`
beside its `.bin` opens on the web and on macOS — and the status line has
carried vertex/material counts since `mat-33d`; neither needed a fresh row.
The other three were still real and became `tut-17`/`tut-19`/`tut-20` below,
alongside `tut-18`, a second latent quirk `ui-37d`'s own text had already
promised "its own row" for.)*

Language: English, per the 2026-09-02 decision on the community; a Russian
version is its own later row.

| id | what | package | size | phase | depends | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

(b) `readiness.dart`'s `MeshChecks` only run on `EditedGeometry`; a freshly imported, unwelded `ImportedGeometry` object reads "ready to export" even when its mesh has real problems `MeshChecks` would catch once welded. **(a) closed 2026-09-15**: `ModelSession.import` now takes an optional `ImportOptions` plus `weld`/`fixNormals`/`triangulate` flags, all defaulting to exactly today's own no-options behaviour (unscaled, up axis `y`, every object left `ImportedGeometry` byte-for-byte); the `import` MCP tool's schema gained matching `unit`/`upAxis`/`weld`/`fixNormals`/`triangulate` fields, so an agent gets the identical choice `import_plan.dart`'s own import screen already offers a person, restricted to only the objects a given import itself just added (the same `only`-set discipline `tut-08`'s own `_applyImportCleanup` already uses). Counted rather than flagged because transactions nest — the count is how many `begin` lines a record owes, which is what keeps the depth a replay sees the depth the caller opened. New coverage: `history_journal_test.dart`'s own attached-journal group (author threading through `run`/`amend`, a transaction bracketing the same way live undo does, `ReplaceDocument` staying invisible to the journal through either door); `tutorial_scenarios_test.dart`'s own case-6 cold-replay test now asserts the person's own `0.35` roughness survives, under `StepAuthor.person`, instead of the silent `0.5` default | mcp + core | M | 3 | mcp-12n, mcp-13n, mcp-10n | closed — any edit run against a shared `ModelHistory`, by a person or an agent, lands on the same recovery journal when one is attached, with the correct author either way; a cold replay after a mixed agent/person session reproduces every edit, not only the agent's own tool calls |

### 2.16 Usability (`ux-`)

Aspect added 2026-09-15 by owner decision, from two documents written the
same day: `docs/modeller-ux-review.md` (four lenses — a beginner, a
professional modeller, an agent over MCP, adaptivity and accessibility —
plus a live run of debug and profile builds on macOS) and
`docs/modeller-ux-comparison.md` (the modeller against Blender, Maya, a
Flutter scene editor and OpenSpace3D, fifteen dimensions). The review's
L1…L21 are findings confirmed on the running app; its §3–§6 tables are
findings by code. Every row below names the finding it closes, so neither
document has to be re-read to act on a row.

**Why a new aspect and not rows in `ui-`/`mcp-`.** The rows those aspects
closed are done by their own acceptance, and the review does not dispute
that: `ui-22` built localisation and translated a quarter of the strings,
`ui-39d` enabled the modes, `mcp-16d` gave the tutorial its UI tools. What
the review found is the distance between "built and tested" and "a person
gets through the first hour" — the gizmo is tested by arithmetic and does
not drag, autosave is tested with a fake clock and never writes. Reopening
closed rows would hide that; this aspect names it.

**The phase column holds a priority tier, not a product phase.** The app
already carries phase-2 and phase-3 screens, and every tier touches all of
them. **P0** — trust and the first hour, closed on the `modeler-ui` branch
before it merges; **P1** — regular irritation, on a new branch from `main`;
**P2** — rough edges; **Play** — its own milestone after P1. Acceptance for
P0 is a test *and* a live run on a macOS build (the review's own method:
`--dart-define=mcpPort=0`, `screencapture -l`); P1 and later — tests.

**Owner decisions 2026-09-15** (twenty questions, recorded in §10): the whole
backlog (P0 + P1 + P2) plus the comparison's borrowings; two navigation
schemes chosen in settings, named for what they do rather than after another
product; three keymap presets at once; G/R/S start either modally or
"arm, then drag", by setting; the gizmo on top with its occluded part dimmed;
Quick Setup once and Home at every launch; the starting cube stops warning
about quads for GLB; a full Material workspace; workspaces Essential/Full
with Essential the default; the agent panel behind a badge; all four MCP
groups (state, strict arguments, explicit targets and batches, onboarding an
agent); Play as a full milestone; touch only as far as the web build needs
it; Russian and English through the whole UI; panel resizing, a command
palette, a message log and docking; loop/ring/linked selection, interactive
Extrude/Bevel, inset/bridge/edge slide, diagnose-and-repair.

**Deliberately not here.** The tablet's and phone's end-to-end scenario —
Animation on the tablet, a draggable sheet, the tablet top bar's overflow —
waits with `ui-21`. Euler-only rotation and the auto-rig's "Custom" stub
(review §4.2, last row) get no row: neither blocks a case of the tutorial.
Not borrowed at all (comparison §4): an arbitrary-code tool for the agent,
hotbox and marking menus, a global user level.

**Done when, by tier.**
*P0:* each of L1–L7 and L9 from the review, re-run on a profile and a
debug build, passes with a screenshot beside the review's own; a MacBook trackpad
orbits; a person opens an STL through Home and gets millimetres and a welded
mesh. *P1:* the review's §5.3 scenario runs over MCP with no blind call;
tutorial cases 1–3 need no workaround in `doc/modeler-tutorial-gaps.md`; the
whole UI switches between Russian and English. *P2:* the review's §9 table
is empty. *Play:* a character from case 4 walks in a game template, and an
edit in the modeller reaches the running game without a restart.

**Size.** P0 — 6 S, 3 M (≈13.5 weeks for one person); P1 — 11 S, 16 M
(≈51); P2 — 7 S (plus one merged), 2 M, 2 L (≈22); Play — 2 S, 1 L (≈7).
≈93.5 weeks in all, by §4.3's weights. Agent tracks under ready tests, as
in §4.2: `ux-22` (key sets), `ux-43` (a test over `tools/list`), `ux-33`/
`ux-34` (guidelines), `ux-10` (a collision test per preset).

The whole top bar, the three shells and the command palette are through; `l10n_no_literal_ui_text_test.dart` holds those five files to it — a literal added to one of them turns it red — with an allow-list that makes every exception name its own reason, and `l10n_tool_strings_test.dart` holds the rail to having both languages and the two ARB files to naming the same keys. **The settings screen went through on 2026-09-17**, and it is the first whole screen rather than a bar: twenty-nine keys, and not only the labels the test can see — the helps under each dropdown and the subtitles under each switch are language too, and a line-by-line check reads none of them, because a `Text(` whose string is on the next line is invisible to it. The language picker's own three names are keys now as well, which closes the exception its doc comment was carrying: it named them in English rather than as endonyms precisely because one hardcoded `Русский` would have been the only Cyrillic in `lib/`, and through the ARB files it is not. The check itself was widened in the same pass and immediately found something: it read one line at a time, so a `Text(` whose string starts on the next line was invisible to it and a sentence broken across two quoted pieces — which is how every paragraph here is written, because the formatter wraps at eighty — showed up as the first piece or not at all. Reading the whole file and joining adjacent pieces turned up the command palette's own "nothing matches", the one real literal left in five files it had been calling clean. Two other catches were strings made only of interpolations, which is not language and has nowhere to go in an ARB file; the skip for those is the rule now rather than the special case of a string that is exactly one `$name`. `settings_test.dart` now pumps with the delegates a real settings dialog always has under it — a screen that reads `AppLocalizations` cannot be pumped in a bare `MaterialApp`, and every test that pumps one this way is a test that would have passed for the wrong reason. **The auto-rig dialog followed on the same day**, and it is the shape the rest of the dialogs will take: the strings come off the context once, at the top of the builder, and the panel and viewport methods take the `AppLocalizations` rather than reaching for a context they do not have — a method on a `State` is not a widget and has no `BuildContext` of its own to ask. Its marker counter is a key with two numbers rather than a sentence built around them. **The five phase-four panels followed** — sculpt, paint, retopology-and-bakes, simulation and render, thirty-three keys, of which two are shared: a brush's Size and Strength are one brush's words whether it is moving clay or paint. Three of their tests had been pumping a panel in a bare `MaterialApp`, which is the same wrong-reason pass `settings_test.dart` had, and they now state `Locale('en')` outright rather than taking the host's — every expectation in them names a word. The twenty tutorial frames were re-taken and not one byte moved, which is what an English build of a screen that now reads its words from a table should do. **The five animation-side panels followed the same day** — weights, morphs, retarget, UV unwrap and the transport bar, thirty-one keys, and the same five-line fix to five more test files that had been pumping a bare `MaterialApp`: a widget test gets away with that for exactly as long as the widget names its own strings. Two `const` lists had to stop being const, which is the honest cost of a looked-up string and is the compiler saying so rather than a surprise. **Then the properties panel itself and both file screens** — forty-nine keys, including the import screen's warning count as a real ICU plural, since Russian has three forms where English has two and an `s` glued on the end is only ever right in one language. Eight more test files got the delegates. **`shortcut_help.dart` went through last, and it was a file-shaped job rather than a line-by-line one**: its rows were a `const List` and a `const Map` at the top level, so nothing in them could read a context. They are functions of an `AppLocalizations` now and `shortcutTable` takes one, which its five call sites had to learn — the three tests among them load the English delegate once and hand it in, which also states the language every expectation in them is written against. `_actionLabels` split in two: the section stays in this file, because saving is not selecting and that is a fact about the application, and the twenty-three names went to the ARB files, read back through a switch for `tool_strings.dart`'s own reason. **The fold that keeps one key from being listed twice had to stay on the label, not the id**, and the test said so within a minute: two different tools that show the same name under the same key are one answer to a reader, and folding on the id instead put both rows on the sheet. Five more panels after it — the material list and its Advanced block, the scene's own light sources, quick setup, the pivot and space chips and the modifier stack. `addableModifiers` takes an `AppLocalizations` now: the five names it hands the picker are what a person reads off the menu, and a top-level function that built them could not have looked them up. Then the lathe, save-as and export-anyway dialogs, the gallery, the budget bars, the console, the metrics overlay and the texture graph — fifty-one keys. **One of those made a test say the opposite of what it had been saying**: `export_anyway_dialog_test.dart` asserted the dialog *stays* English under `Locale('ru')`, and said in its own name why — there was no ARB entry for it. A test that still expected English would have been the thing keeping the dialog untranslated, so it reads the Russian now and carries the account of the reversal. `texture_graph_panel.dart` gave up a quoted string inside an interpolation (`'#${value ?? '—'}'`): it reads as the end of the string to every scanner that is not a Dart parser, this check included, so the em dash is built above the widget and the string interpolates one name. Then the game preview, the agent panel, the legal screen, the status line, the environment panel, the animation panel and the job button — eleven keys and a great many arrow bodies turned into blocks, which is what a widget that used to name its own strings costs when it starts looking them up. Seven more test files got the delegates. **Closed 2026-09-17.** The last pass took the tail — the crash card, the post and play screens, the operation card, the material studio, the constraints, the clip tracks and library, the bone map, the animation bottom, the start screen, the roomy dialog, the name field, the mesh health panel, the LOD bar, the actions list, the cache strip and the two wirings — and `translated` stopped being a list somebody adds to: it is now **every `.dart` file under `lib/` except the `l10n` output and `tools.dart`**, two hundred and thirty-three of them, generated into the test rather than typed. A new file is inside the check the moment it exists, which is the thing a growing allow-list could never promise. Widening it that far found five more the earlier passes had missed, `ready_parts.dart`'s own light names among them, and a second quoted string inside an interpolation in `modifier_fields.dart` — the axis letter is read out above the string now, the same fix `texture_graph_panel.dart` took. **And the acceptance's other half is a test now, not a claim**: the source check holds that no file *names* an English string, which is not the same as no English being on the screen. `properties_panel_test.dart` pumps the panel in every ready mode under `Locale('ru')` and fails on any Latin word it can find, with an allow-list that is a decision per entry — an axis letter, a primitive's own kind, a profile's name, and `ProjectSelection.says`, which lives in a package with no interface and is English by the same rule every other `says` is. ### Л. The gallery

**Asked for on 2026-09-17, and the licence question decided with it.** A
modeller that starts every scene from a cube asks a person to model a chair
before they can look at a room. The gallery is where a scene gets its props:
models this repository ships, and models from outside it. Everything from
outside carries its licence on the card and out through the export, and the
list shows CC0 first — the owner's own call, made because "free models" and
"models you may ship without crediting anybody" are different sets and a
gallery that blurs them is a gallery that gets somebody sued.

| id | what | package | size | phase | depends | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| gal-01 | **The catalogue**: `GalleryItem` (id, name, category, licence, source, a thumbnail), `GalleryLicence` naming what each one demands of an export, `GallerySource` as the one interface a source implements — local recipes, a cached download, an API. Licence is not optional and has no default: a source that cannot say what an item is licensed under does not offer it Built: `GalleryLicence` as a class with const instances rather than an enum — a licence this build has never heard of is a real thing a source can name; `GalleryItem`, which cannot be constructed without one and has no `unknown` to fall back to; `GallerySource` as the one interface, whose `list` answers rather than throws so a source that is down is a row saying so instead of an exception that takes the other sources' items with it; `refuseItem`, which turns away an item under a licence that asks for a credit and names nobody to credit — the alternative is an export that claims to attribute and writes a blank line; `freeFirst`, a sort rather than a filter, because hiding CC-BY would leave somebody who wanted one with no way to know it was there; and `RecipeSource`, the one source that is always reachable and needs no licence checking (`gallery_catalogue_test.dart`) | app | S | P2 | — | an item with no licence cannot be constructed; the list is ordered CC0 first |
| gal-02 | **Recipes — models that are code.** `recipes.dart` in `flutter3d_mesh`: lighting (floor, pendant, table, sconce), furniture (chair, stool, table, bench, shelf, cabinet), tableware (mug, vase, plate, bottle), architecture (door, window). Built from the lathe, the cuboid and a join rather than stored as files: nothing to license, nothing to download, and every dimension a number a person can change. Real measurements — a seat at 450, a table at 740 — because props that each look right alone and wrong beside each other are props nobody builds a room from Built: `recipes.dart` — `Recipe` (id, category, a sentence, a build), `RecipeCategory`'s four sections, sixteen models and `joinMeshes`, which renumbers each part's live vertices through its own transform rather than concatenating faces that then name somebody else's vertices. A stool's legs sit two millimetres higher than their own axis says, because a tilted cylinder stands on the rim of its foot rather than the middle of it, and a prop sunk into the floor is a prop with no shadow gap (`recipes_test.dart`) | mesh | M | P2 | — | every recipe builds a mesh `validate()` accepts; two calls give the same mesh vertex for vertex |
| gal-03 | **The gallery screen**: a grid of cards by category, a search, a licence chip on every card, and an insert that goes through `ImportInto` so it is one undo step and lands beside what is already open rather than replacing it Built: `GalleryScreen` — a grid by category with a search, a licence chip on every card including the free ones (a chip that appeared only on the awkward items would train people not to read it), and a filter named `No credit needed` rather than `CC0 only`, because the person who has to decide is the one least likely to know what CC0 means. A source that cannot answer is a row saying which one, never an empty grid. `insertIntoProject` is pure — a project in, a project out — and a built model goes in as `EditedGeometry` rather than through a file, because a round trip through glTF would lose the topology that makes a recipe worth having. One `ReplaceDocument`, so an insert is one undo and the scene underneath survives it (`gallery_screen_test.dart`) | app | M | P2 | gal-01, gal-02, ux-06 | inserting from the gallery is one undo; the document that was open is still open |
| gal-04 | **Sources outside this repository**: Smithsonian Open Access and the Khronos sample assets (both CC0), Poly Pizza and Sketchfab (mixed, mostly CC-BY). Fetched on demand and cached on disk; the filter shows CC0 by default and says plainly what turning it off means. A source that is unreachable is a row that says so, never an empty grid Built: `RemoteSource` — a catalogue fetched, parsed and cached for a day, taking a `Fetch` rather than owning an HTTP client so the same code runs on the web and in a test with no socket; a cache entry nothing can read is fetched past rather than failed on, and nothing is cached from a body that would not parse. `khronosSamples` maps the glTF sample assets' own `model-index.json`, reading the licence and the artist per model: an unknown licence is skipped rather than assumed, because marking a restricted model free is how an export ships without a credit it owed. **Smithsonian, Poly Pizza and Sketchfab are named and not wired**: each needs a key and a live response, none has been read from this repository, and `outsideSourcesNotWiredYet` says what each adapter will need instead of shipping a guessed shape that shows an empty grid with no error anybody can act on. The default list holds nothing that needs a key, so the "could not be reached" row still means something the day a catalogue really is down (`remote_gallery_test.dart`) | app | L | P2 | gal-01 | the web build lists and inserts; a source that times out leaves the rest of the grid usable |
| gal-05 | **The export carries what a licence demands**: an item under CC-BY writes its attribution into the exported file's own metadata and into a `CREDITS.txt` beside it, named per model. CC0 writes nothing, which is the point of preferring it Built: `ModelCredit` on `ModelObject` — on the object rather than on the project, so deleting the chair takes the obligation with it and an export credits what is actually in the file; written to `.f3dproj` only where there is one, and read back as no credit at all when the author is missing, because a line that claims to attribute and names nobody is worse than no line. `creditsIn`/`creditsFile`/`creditsLine` in core: one entry per author and title however many objects carry it, a stable order so two exports of one document agree, and null rather than an empty file for a project that owes nothing. `creditFor` records the obligation at the insert rather than working it out at the export — a catalogue can change under a document, and the obligation belongs to the moment somebody took the model (`credits_test.dart`) | app + core | M | P2 | gal-04, ux-18 | a project with one CC-BY model exports with that credit and no other; an all-CC0 project exports no credits file |
| gal-07 | **The catalogue behind one endpoint of our own** (asked 2026-09-17). `cloud/server` answers `/gallery/index.json` with every item it can offer and `/gallery/model/<source>/<id>` with the bytes, so the keys Smithsonian, Poly Pizza and Sketchfab want live on the server rather than inside a shipped application — which is the only place they can live at all, since an application anybody can download cannot hold a secret. The server is also where a licence is checked once rather than in every client, and where a slow catalogue is cached for everybody instead of per machine Built: `gallery_routes.dart` mounts the two endpoints under `/gallery/`, and `gallery_catalogue.dart` is what they read from — an interface, so the routes' own rules are checkable without a network, with `FixedCatalogue` as both the test's catalogue and the answer a deploy with no keys gives. The index spells a licence out rather than naming its id, because a client that has to keep its own table of what `cc-by-4.0` means is a client that ships an export without a credit the day that table goes stale, and it names what it could not reach instead of counting it, since nobody can act on "3 sources failed". `kIndexCacheControl` lets a client keep the list five minutes where the modeller's own cache is a day: this copy is the shared one. The model endpoint is a proxy on purpose — a direct URL would put the key back in every client — and it serves only ids the index offers, since a proxy that fetched whatever it was asked for would be an open relay with our own key on it (`gallery_routes_test.dart`) | cloud | M | P2 | gal-04 | the modeller lists and inserts with no key of its own; a source the server cannot reach leaves the rest of the index answerable; an item whose licence the server cannot read is not in the index |
| gal-06 | **The agent inserts from the gallery**: `gallery.list {category?, licence?}` and `gallery.insert {id}`, answering with the ids the insert made — the same `structuredContent` every other tool answers with Built: `gallery.list` answers one line per item, each starting with the id `gallery.insert` takes and each naming the licence — an agent that picks a CC-BY model and never read a licence is an export that owes somebody a credit nothing warned about; `category` and `licence` narrow it the way the screen's own chips do. `gallery.insert` runs the same `insertIntoProject` the screen runs, as one `ReplaceDocument`, and refuses an unknown id by naming the tool the ids come from rather than only saying no. ## 3. Dependencies between aspects

Text references from the plans ("mesh: EditMesh.toMeshData") are resolved
into ids. Where several aspects wrote the same thing, the table below says
whose implementation stays and whose id is marked "⇢" in the tables.

### 3.1 One implementation instead of several

| Thing | Who wrote it | Where it lives | Who becomes a consumer |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pure engine vocabulary packages | mesh-03 (`flutter3d_geometry`), doc-01 (the earlier working name for one shared package), p0-09 (`flutter3d_model`), qa-03, rel-03 | **two packages** (B1 [В1] closed by the 2026-09-09 owner decision): `flutter3d_geometry` — mesh-03 (`MeshData`, `VertexLayout`, `Shape`/`LatheShape`, tangents, `morph_target`, `CpuMesh`, `math/intersections`, `Ray`, `TriangleBvh`); `flutter3d_formats` — doc-01 (`ModelDocument`, `SurfaceMaterial`, `MaterialDocument`/`MaterialHint`, `lighting_model`, `model_loader`'s synchronous half, gltf/obj/f3d/ktx2/stl decoders, writers `F3dWriter`/`GltfWriter`/`ObjWriter`); `formats → geometry`, `mesh → geometry`, `model_core → both`, `flutter3d` re-exports both | mesh-13/14/17/20 (geometry), doc-11/12/19 (formats), fmt-01..09/12/15 (writers in `formats`; only fmt-13 and fmt-14 stay in the engine), mat-03 (formats), fmt-29d (`fbx → formats`) |
| Registering packages in the scanner/CI/documents | mesh-00, doc-02, ui-01, qa-02, qa-04, doc-30, rel-02, rel-03, rel-07, qa-16 | qa-02 (the lists), qa-04 (`ci.sh`), rel-02 (pubspec), rel-07 (documents); mesh-00/doc-02/ui-01 — one package each | everything |

### 3.3 Added during synthesis

Three references didn't resolve into any item; items were created for them.
Nine items with an `-n` suffix (mat-04a-n, mat-09n, doc-11a-n, anim-31a-n,
p0-13n, ui-30n, ui-31n, ui-32n, qa-19n) were added by the 2026-09-09
critique, five with a `-d` suffix (doc-31d, ui-33d, ui-34d, rel-19d,
fmt-29d) — by 2026-09-09 owner decisions; all sit in their own aspect's
tables.

| id | what | package | size | phase | depends | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| syn-01 *(added during synthesis)* | `ParamHint` — its own sealed type in core (Int / Double / Bool / Enum / Vector3, with `step`, `unit`, a range) for the operation card; `ModelCommand.hints` on commands with parameters (Extrude, LoopCut, MergeByDistance, MoveElements, AddPrimitive, AddLathe). Without it ui-09 can't build controls, and doc-05 only talks about `arguments`. The type was decided by the 2026-09-09 critique (D4/F2 [Г4/Ж2]): `MaterialHint` doesn't fit — it only has `RangeHint(double, step)`/`ColorHint`/`TextureHint`/`EnumHint`, no integers (`LoopCut.cuts`), no flags (`Extrude.individual`), no units, and the file pulls in `LightingModel` | core | S | 1 | doc-05 | every command with a numeric argument has a hint; test: `hints`' keys ⊂ `arguments`' keys; `LoopCut.cuts` is an `IntHint`, `Extrude.individual` a `BoolHint` |
| syn-02 *(added during synthesis)* | Mockups for the two phase-1 screens missing from the handoff: import with checks and export with checks (the handoff README itself requires them before the phase starts; ui-16/17 and fmt-12 describe the data, not the look). The same `.dc.html` style, the same token table. **Closed 2026-09-17 by reading the archive rather than by drawing anything**: both screens are already there, and have been since the 11 September addendum — `Экраны редактора - дополнение.dc.html` carries «Экран 21 · Импорт: отчёт и лимиты» (a 1180 × 720 modal: preview with bounds, content counters, units and up axis, mesh preparation, the decoder's own report) and «Экран 22 · Экспорт с проверками» (1080 × 720: format, profile, contents, the check list with its "Показать" link, four budget bars, and a warning that changes the button's label rather than blocking it), in the same `.dc.html` and the same token vocabulary as the original nineteen. `README-дополнение.md` lists them both. What this pass actually owed was the second half of the acceptance — ui-16 and ui-17 naming the mockup each is built from — and those two rows now do, which is also what clears the `⚠` they were carrying: it meant "the screen this is built from does not exist", and it does | design | S | 0 | — | two screens in the handoff archive; ui-16/17 reference them |
| syn-03 *(added during synthesis)* | `ProjectProfile.fps` and `frameSnap` — a time base for animation in the profile (anim-04 rounds a frame as `round(time·fps)`, anim-13 reads fps); doc-13 doesn't have the field | core | S | 3 | doc-13 | a JSON round trip; `KeyTable` reads fps from the profile |
